`src/nwqlib/resources/records.py::CLIFFORD_ANGLES` and `T_ANGLES` are the conventional angles that name a one-qubit phase or single-axis rotation. A phase or rotation by 0, ±pi/2, ±pi, ±3pi/2 or ±2pi is a Clifford gate, and one by ±pi/4 is one T gate up to Clifford gates, in each case up to global phase. The resource fold's primitive recipes (`fold._Fold.primitive`) and the QHD rotation-count formulas (`src/nwqlib/algorithms/qhd/resources.py::rotation_population` and `_binary_population`) classify every emitted angle with these sets. The comparison is exact on the binary64 value, with no reduction modulo 2 pi and no tolerance, so a nearby arbitrary angle is never counted as a Clifford or T gate. The sets are an angle convention, not tuned values. Revisit when a new primitive or rotation gate uses another angle convention.

## Runs and archives

These sizes and windows govern the run log (the file in which a Run records its rows), a Run's readouts and its saved files.

### Local journal scalar admission

`nwqlib._run_journal._json_bound` bounds the size of a JSON value from the escaped UTF-8 string lengths, the decimal lengths of integers, the exact punctuation and an upper bound of 32 bytes per finite binary64 value, before the value is encoded. It traverses Record fields and rejects nesting deeper than 128. `Run._write` encodes each row change once, after a walk of its containers that applies the same key, scalar and nesting rules and stops at Records, and, except for the rows of work already started, checks its measured UTF-8 size against the remaining data limit. FrozenArray values use their existing dtype, shape and base64 JSON form. The check computes the encoded size of such an array from its metadata and includes the shape list in the nesting limit. The limit on saved data bounds the encoded bytes of new work and new records, while the method that produces the data checks its live arrays and serialization workspace. The float allowance exceeds the longest decimal representation of a finite binary64 value, while the depth cap is an untuned recursion boundary. The encoded sizes are checked again after encoding. These size bounds do not estimate CPU or RSS. The bytes set aside before circuits run use the same bound for the declared readout and values. The `KernelApplication` records of a classical routine (a classical computation that a backend runs in place of a circuit), which the kernel declares in `SelectedKernel.application_bytes`, are set aside with the routine's outputs. The other records of an execution that has started, such as the job text of its observation, its timing, provider messages and failure text, are not set aside. They are counted at their encoded size when they are saved, and they are saved even when the Run's total then exceeds `max_data_bytes`. [SQLite's default single-row length limit](https://www.sqlite.org/limits.html) is 1,000,000,000 bytes, independent of `max_data_bytes` and subject to the installed SQLite build.

The run log, the archive readers and the backend adapters use these further size constants. None of them changes which data are saved.

| Constant | Value | Site | Reason and revisit condition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `_run_journal.py::LocalJournal.commit` and `_prepared_execution.py::Run._saved_payload`, binary block size | 1 MiB (`PAYLOAD_BLOCK_BYTES`), whatever the data limit | `src/nwqlib/_run_journal.py`, `src/nwqlib/_prepared_execution.py` | Keeps each saved value far below SQLite's per-value limit and bounds the buffer of each block-wise read. Reloading must read with the same block size. Revisit condition: A changed SQLite value limit or read buffer requirement, in both sites together. |
| `artifacts.py::_check_finite_blocks`, finiteness-check block when an array is saved (`ArtifactStore._publish`), and `artifacts.py::probability_readout`, block of its finiteness and nonnegativity checks and of its `math.fsum` total | `_FINITE_CHECK_ENTRIES = 65536` entries, or one leading-axis slice when that slice is longer | `src/nwqlib/artifacts.py` | Bounds the Boolean mask of `np.isfinite` (and of `values >= 0`) to 64 KiB, and the list of Python floats that `math.fsum` reads to one block, instead of one entry per array entry. Untuned. Any block length gives the same accept or reject decision and the same correctly rounded total. Revisit condition: A measured cost of saving arrays that a different block length reduces. |
| `_prepared_execution.py::_merged_associations`, provider association check | 32 bytes per item | `src/nwqlib/_prepared_execution.py` | A cheap check before `_merged_associations` builds its lookup tables. The saved items already fit the data cap at more than 32 bytes each (an attempt UUID alone has 36 characters), so the check rejects only an oversized provider report, before the loop reads it. Revisit condition: A different format of attempt identifiers. |
| Adapters reading saved or returned JSON text (`backends/ibm_runtime.py`, `ionq.py`, `nexus.py`) | `4 * len(text)` | `src/nwqlib/backends/ibm_runtime.py`, `src/nwqlib/backends/ionq.py`, `src/nwqlib/backends/nexus.py` | UTF-8 encodes at most 4 bytes per character, and the text has at most `len(text)` JSON nodes. The value is checked against the Run's data cap before parsing. It bounds the text, not the memory of the parsed objects. Revisit condition: A parse whose object memory must also be bounded. |
| IonQ writing or reading a saved QPY payload (`backends/ionq.py`) | `2 * max_input_bytes` before a QPY dump, `2 * len(payload)` before a saved payload is decoded, each against the Run's data limit | `src/nwqlib/backends/ionq.py` | Untuned allowance for the encoded bytes plus one payload-sized copy (the `getvalue()` bytes of the QPY buffer or the stream that `qpy.load` reads). Revisit condition: A changed serializer or decoder that keeps more copies. |
| IBM writing or reading a saved QPY payload of `b` bytes (`backends/_qpy_buffer.py::run_qpy_buffer`, `backends/ibm_runtime.py::restore_native`, `restore_template`) | `2b`, with what the Run holds, against the Method's `max_bytes` or the physical memory, before the buffer takes the bytes and before a saved payload is decoded. `b` against the bytes left of `max_data_bytes`, when it is written. While it is written, `b` is the buffer's next length, the larger of its length and the end of the write | `src/nwqlib/backends/_qpy_buffer.py`, `src/nwqlib/backends/ibm_runtime.py` | Memory and stored data are separate quantities, so each has its own limit, and `max_data_bytes=None` leaves the memory comparison in force. `2b` is an untuned coarse allowance for the encoded bytes plus one payload-sized copy (the `getvalue()` bytes of the buffer or the stream that `qpy.load` reads), not a bound on process memory, and SDK temporaries and the spare capacity of `BytesIO` are outside it. Each payload takes a new key, so all of its bytes are new stored data. Qiskit 2.6.0's `qpy.dump` hands over a whole circuit in one write, so the comparison cannot stop the SDK's own serialization. `Run._write` counts the actual bytes when the record commits. Revisit condition: A changed serializer or decoder that keeps more copies, or a Qiskit version that writes a circuit in pieces. |
| `backends/ibm_runtime.py::sampler_decode_bytes` | `S*(9*Q+w+32)` bytes for `S` shots, `w=sum(w_r)` bits and `Q=sum(ceil(w_r/8))` packed bytes per shot | `src/nwqlib/backends/ibm_runtime.py` | A coarse estimate of the arrays of joining and decoding one Sampler PUB, checked once before the join. Qiskit's `BitArray.concatenate_bits` unpacks each register to one byte per bit of its padded bytes, `8*Q` per shot, joins the `w` bits and packs them into at most `Q` bytes. Up to 64 bits, the joined integers, their gathered classical order and `np.unique` take about four 8-byte arrays per shot, covered by the 32. With Qiskit 2.6.0 and NumPy 2.5.2, the measured array peak of one decode was 43 to 83 percent of the estimate for one to eight registers of 1 to 64 bits in total and 1,000 or 100,000 shots. Above 64 bits, the bit-string count dictionary is Python objects and is not included. Revisit condition: A changed join, count representation or NumPy unique implementation. |

### Readout allowances

| Constant | Value | Site | Reason and revisit condition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Probability trajectory JSON headers | Maximum over valid dense and sparse encodings | `src/nwqlib/_prepared_execution.py::_probability_point_metadata` | The bytes set aside for each point cover its ProbabilityArrays record, the growth of its saved-array row and its payload-reference rows, using the field-tree bound of the run log and a job identifier of the length that the backend states (`native_job_id_length`). Sparse entry counts are bounded by `(2**q - 1)//(W + 1)`, with `W = ceil(q/64)`. Binary values have their own `8*2**q` bytes set aside. Revisit when the record schema, the encoding choice or the sequence of saving arrays changes. |
| Projected reduction workspace | `49*2**n + 320*M + 2**17` bytes for one `projected_moments` point with n coordinates and M Pauli terms of the observable, without the `320*M` part for a mass-only reduction | `src/nwqlib/_quantum_readout.py::_projected_workspace`, checked by `src/nwqlib/_prepared_execution.py::_admit_trajectory_work` | The reductions run one at a time after the simulation while the kept saves are held, so the kept saves (on Aer the saved states, probabilities and expectations, otherwise the saved states) plus the largest workspace must fit `simulator_memory_mb` before native preparation. With N = 2**n and d <= N physical entries, the noncontiguous gather of `amplitudes._slice` holds index and counter arrays with their shift temporaries (32d bytes) and the gathered slice (16d), which stays live. The moment then holds the zero-padded framed vector with the Pauli action's output and finite-mask reserve (33N, `pauli_action_requirements` for a complex input). The peak is 16d + 33N <= 49N. While the action runs, each term keeps the packed table (32 bytes) and the framed copy that `PauliTerms._snapshot` makes (32). `apply_terms` groups the terms in Python containers, a dict from each flip key to a list of member indices and a list of rotated complex coefficients. On CPython 3.12, with 64-bit flip keys and one group per term, tracemalloc measured these containers at 760 term counts from 1 to 300,000, chosen around the counts where the dict grows. At the measured counts from 6 to 300,000 terms their peak is at most 254 bytes per term, and for 1 to 5 terms at most 1,282 bytes in all, whose excess over 254 bytes per term fits in the 2**17 part. The 254 bytes and the two 32-byte tables give 318 bytes per term, rounded to 320. The framed coefficient copy, identity mask and saved real parts are released before the action. The action's grouped tiles (at most 98,304 bytes) and the mass tiles (under 2**15) fit in 2**17. Python objects of the parsed parameters and the process's resident memory are not counted. A tracemalloc measurement of the reduction stays below this bound for observables with 32 flip masks and with about 900 (`tests/test_quantum_readout.py`). Revisit when `reduce_scaled`, `scaled_projected_moment`, `amplitudes._slice`, the grouping of `apply_terms` or the Python version changes. |
| `amplitudes.py::admit_materialization` | `16*N+192*M` bytes | `src/nwqlib/amplitudes.py` | The complex128 state vector from the backend has N entries. M requested entries require bounded gather, normalization and physical-scaling buffers. The bound of 192 bytes per output covers the known arrays that exist at the same time. Wider mass projection adds `16*W+8*k` bytes for a contiguous slice or `80*W+8*k` for an indexed slice. This is the check before a requested amplitude readout, not a check of a scientific property. Revisit condition: The reduction buffers or the backend's state representation change. |

### Runtime seed range

`MAX_RUNTIME_SEED = 2**32 - 1` in `src/nwqlib/core/planning.py` bounds the backend seed recorded in `RuntimeOptions`. Method and backend PCG64 streams derive from the chosen SeedSequence. Draws fixed before running are saved in order, and resume restores the saved state and pending draw. There is no arithmetic offset or wraparound. Revisit with a changed backend seed domain.

### Readout count representation

`_validation.MAX_COUNT`, also named `nwqlib.execution.MAX_COUNT`, is `2**63 - 1`. This is a representation boundary, not a statistical or workload tolerance. `ObservationChunk.histogram()` returns counts as int64 weights. A counts declaration requires `1 <= shots <= 2**63 - 1`, and an `ObservationChunk` whose counts are not each at most that maximum, with an exact total equal to its returned shots and within its requested shots, is rejected on every construction and load path. An int64 sum of one validated chunk's counts therefore cannot overflow. Totals across several executions need an overflow-safe accumulation. Revisit with a wider integer weight representation.

### Selected amplitude mass consistency

Amplitude readout from a backend and the amplitude and classical-routine results of QLS use the shared `nwqlib._validation.NUMERICAL_RELATION_RTOL` convention, written `w` below. A negative mass is rejected. For algorithm mass `a` and physical mass `p`, the subset check requires `p-a <= w*max(1,a)`. A Plan selecting the whole branch uses the two-sided difference. Raw values remain unchanged. When its preparation record (the record that the backend writes about the circuit) supplies a `saved_state_probability_window`, an amplitude readout also requires each normalized mass to be at most one plus that window (`ObservationChunk.validate_unit_bound`). The available window is never below `w`. An unassessed saved-state correction leaves only the nonnegativity check. Classical-routine QLS masses must be at most `1+w`. The window `w` is a numerical input convention, not a simulator error theorem, recovery scale or verification threshold. The comparison adds no norm evaluation. An exact scalar QLS result reports the masses of its saved reduction, whose subset relation carries the classical mass allowance of `_quantum_readout.validate_saved_masses`, and a sampled QLS result compares nested integer counts. On a Plan that selects the whole branch, both report one mass for the algorithm and physical subsets together.

### Projected scalar reduction

The exact projected reduction scans masses in tiles of at most 1024 amplitudes (`tile <= 1024`), and its workspace is the projected reduction workspace above. `_quantum_readout.pair_value` accepts saved scalar exponents in `[-8192, 8192]`, a derived bound for finite binary64 inputs and accepted lengths below `2**51`, not a precision cutoff. The projected reduction resolves only the `amplitude-derived masses` label of the preparation record that produced its data. Its own classical allowance is `a_X = gamma(2*n_X+1)` with `U_X = 32*n_X*eta*M_hat_X/(1-(2*n_X+1)*u)` for each declared outcome set X, where `u=2**-53` and `eta=2**-1074`. With the resulting qualified budget delta of the backend state, `validate_saved_masses` uses the complete-mass window `2*delta+delta**2+a_F*(1+delta)**2+U_F`. If another assumption of the backend derivation remains unavailable, it uses the probability-window convention `omega+a_F*(1+omega)+U_F`. The reducer registry supplies the resolved budget when the saved simulator states are collected, and LCHS and QLS use the same resolution when they save their results. Revisit these with a different mass routine or a wider domain of backend states.

### Binary inference representation

Binary counts are Python integers with no upper limit, and accumulation adds them exactly. The family size F is an integer too, and the failure probability `alpha = delta/F` of each member is the exact quotient rounded once to binary64, so F beyond the binary64 range gives a subnormal alpha, or zero and an unavailable interval with its reason. The point estimate (n0-n1)/n is rounded once from the exact ratio, and the exact sample-mean variance is saved as a `Rational`, or reported unavailable with its reason when its reduced integers exceed what a saved record holds. The Hoeffding radius sqrt(2L/n) is evaluated in binary64, from logarithms when n exceeds the binary64 range. When the radius falls below the resolution of the mean, which no n up to 2**53 reaches, the exact endpoints are rounded outward with the radius raised to at least the smallest subnormal, so the interval keeps a positive width. As for every n, this is not a certified arithmetic bound. Beta posterior construction checks that positive prior/count inputs and tail probabilities survive binary64 addition/complement, so a count above 2**53 gives an unavailable Beta interval with its reason. Revisit with another explicitly supported numerical representation. The `BinaryReadoutMitigation.minimum_contrast` value is a caller-chosen conditioning threshold (default 0.05), not an inferred calibration-quality guarantee.

`nwqlib.evidence.binary.MAX_BETA_SHAPE_TOTAL` is `5e11`. When the posterior shape total a+b exceeds it, the equal-tail Beta interval is unavailable, and the posterior mean and variance remain available. Below it the lower endpoint is SciPy's `betaincinv(a, b, alpha/2)` and the upper endpoint is `betainccinv(a, b, alpha/2)`, which inverts the complement 1-I_x(a,b) directly because rounding 1-alpha/2 changes the upper tail by up to 2**-54. The accuracy criterion is an endpoint error of at most 1e-3 posterior standard deviations. For a near-normal posterior an endpoint error of eps standard deviations changes the tail mass alpha/2 by a relative amount of about eps*phi(z)/Phi(-z), where z > 0 solves Phi(-z) = alpha/2. That ratio is 2.3 at alpha/2 = 0.025 and 8.4 at 2**-54, the smallest accepted tail, so for such a posterior the criterion keeps each tail mass within 1 percent of alpha/2.

The threshold is measured. The reference integrates the Beta density with mpmath 1.3.0 at 50 digits over one-standard-deviation panels and refines each quantile by safeguarded Newton steps. Where `mpmath.betainc` also runs, the two agree to better than 1e-26 in relative tail mass. For Beta(a, 1), whose lower quantile is (alpha/2)**(1/a), the reference matches that closed form within 1e-22 standard deviations up to a = 1e13. The fixed grid takes, at each a+b, zero fractions n0/n = 0.5, 0.3, 0.7, 0.1, 0.9, 1e-3 and 0.999, the counts n0 = 0, n0 = 10, n1 = 0 and n1 = 10, the uniform prior and, for n0 = 0, also the Jeffreys prior, with tails alpha/2 = 0.025, 1e-6, 1e-12 and 6e-17 and both endpoints. The dense sample adds 300 zero fractions, 150 log-uniform in [1e-6, 0.5] and their mirror images, with the same tails and the uniform prior. SciPy 1.18.1, NumPy 2.5.2 and Python 3.12.14 on macOS arm64 gave these largest endpoint errors, in posterior standard deviations:

| a+b | Fixed grid | Dense sample |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1e10 | 4.1e-7 | |
| 1e11 | 1.4e-5 | |
| 5e11 | 2.6e-4 | 4.4e-4 |
| 1e12 | 4.3e-4 | 1.2e-3 |
| 2e12 | 5.1e-4 | 2.0e-3 |
| 5e12 | 1.1e-3 | |
| 1e13 | 4.1e-3 | |

The error grows about in proportion to a+b, and the largest dense-sample errors are about 10(a+b)u with u = 2**-53. That scale is consistent with rounding of order (a+b)u in the exponent a*ln(x) + b*ln(1-x) of the incomplete beta prefactor, an explanation inferred from the scaling alone. 5e11 is the largest measured total whose samples stayed below 1e-3, with a factor of 2.3 to spare. On Ubuntu aarch64 with SciPy 1.18.1, a retest found a largest endpoint error of 2.58e-4 posterior standard deviations at a+b = 5e11, so the same 1e-3 criterion holds there. A sampled maximum does not bound every input. Revisit when SciPy changes its incomplete beta inverse, or with a routine that evaluates I_x(a,b) in extended precision.

### Saved JSON and analysis-origin admission

`ArchiveFiles.read_json` checks the file-byte allowance of a folder opened with one before reading the JSON. The run log checks its cumulative saved bytes before reading rows. Its 128-level nesting limit applies when writing a row and bounds the writer’s recursive size calculation.

`capture_analysis_origin(max_dependencies=64)` bounds the complete supplied list or tuple of package names, including duplicates, before deduplication and version lookups. The fixed `nwqlib` and `pydantic` names are additional. External code that records an analysis origin can choose a larger explicit bound for a known list of dependencies. These are installed metadata queries, not imports or scientific execution.

#### Record identity JSON

`src/nwqlib/core/records.py::Record.content_id` encodes the record's fields as JSON text, the identity JSON, and hashes its UTF-8 bytes. A nested record enters the identity JSON as its type and content hash, which it computes and keeps first, and a `FrozenArray` as the SHA-256 digest of its elements, read in place. The identity JSON therefore grows with a record's own fields other than arrays and with its number of nested records, not with their contents. `model_dump` shares the record's scalar objects, so the hash holds two copies of the JSON beyond the record's own data, and each nested record afterwards keeps its content hash, a 71-character string. The record does not know its caller's `max_bytes`, so no check applies there. An ordinary JSON dump, such as `Plan.to_record` or `Result.save`, copies the containers of each nested record once more, because every record's serialization is wrapped to write that reference. For a q = 6 Pauli Expectation Plan this raised the peak per term from 200 to 854 bytes in `to_record` and from 654 to 1,684 bytes in `Result.save` (Python 3.12.14, Pydantic 2.13.5, macOS arm64). The copy is transient, and saving counts written file bytes, not Python objects. Revisit when a workflow's save becomes memory bound, or when Pydantic can choose a serializer per call without inferring the result again. The table lists the records whose JSON grows with the problem size, the check that bounds each one and whether that bound also covers the identity JSON.

| Records | Size grows with | Bounded by | Identity JSON against that bound |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `evidence/energy_shift.py::EnergyEndpoint` (`table_keys`, `table_values`) | M Pauli rows, or nnz or D² scanned matrix entries | `_operator_table` | Covered. The identity JSON of the endpoint, or of a record that holds it, contains the digests of the tables, not their elements. |
| `ir/records.py::Program` with its nodes, settings and bindings, and `blocks/records.py::SelectedConstruction.selections`, one per Program signature | Definitions, signatures, measurement settings and bindings | `src/nwqlib/ir/expressions.py::AdmissionLimits`, no default limit on declarations or kept fields | The declaration count is independent of D and M. At b=3 basis states, q=20 qubits and 8083 Hamiltonian terms, a sampled FixedGCIM plan with G=1285 measurement groups has 11,565 circuits and 25,731 declarations. Singleton grouping has at most 161,691 declarations. Explicit field and checking-work limits apply independently. |
| Pauli-term reconstructions and readout specs: `ExpectationReconstruction`, `FixedGCIMReconstruction`, `QPEReconstruction`, `LCHSReconstruction`, `QLSReconstruction`, `AdaptReconstruction` with `AdaptPoolMember`, and `core/planning.py::ObservableEstimateSpec` and `ObservationSpec` | M terms of an accepted Pauli operator, and for ADAPT its Hamiltonian and pool terms | The check of the operator input, which adds `S=3*max(2q+120,150)+112` bytes per term (`src/nwqlib/operators/inputs.py::_pauli_identity_bytes`) to `q+4L` per label term, `4L` per mask term and `5L` per `SparsePauliOp` term. The Fermion mapping and the final Pauli expansion of `FactorizedOperatorProduct` include `S` in their formulas. | Covered for QPE, the ADAPT Hamiltonian and pool, Expectation without sampled counts, the labels of grouped sampled Expectation and QLS with vector or exact scalar output. The paragraph under this table gives the Lanczos case, the derivation of `S` and `J`, and the measurements. |
| `execution.py::ObservationChunk`, `ObservationView`, `ExecutionTrace` and the contribution lists of `core/analysis.py::Result` | Executions, their count bins, and their probability array manifests and summaries | The output bytes set aside before each execution (encoded readout declaration, declared values and eight bytes per possible outcome of a probability array), for a trajectory each point's one-point declaration, fixed chunk header and values (`_prepared_execution.trajectory_reservation`), counted against `ExecutionLimits.max_data_bytes`, the item and payload check before the backend circuit is prepared (`_prepared_execution._admit_readout`) and `max_total_circuits` | Proportional. The bytes set aside bound a chunk's declared values and fixed header. Its other fields are counted at their encoded size when it is saved and are not bounded by `max_data_bytes`. Arrays are separate saved files whose manifests hold digests only. |
| `qhd/constrained_records.py::AugmentedLagrangianRecord` with its `ALIteration` rounds | Rounds times the constraints and variables of the problem | `src/nwqlib/algorithms/qhd/constrained.py::_admit_layer_work`. Each accepted round evaluates f and every constraint at a point of d coordinates for at least `(1 + m)(1 + d)` units with m constraints, and saves at most `6 m + 2 d + 91` JSON values outside a nested refinement under `inequality_form="phr"`, counting nested content hashes, versions and unknown-count reasons (70 to 92 values for one to four inequality constraints on one to three variables), and at most `29 m + 3 d + 100` for m >= 1, or `3 d + 102` for m = 0, with the inequality representation of the other forms ([augmented-Lagrangian record size](#augmented-lagrangian-record-size)) | Proportional. The record holds at most 91 JSON values per unit of layer work counted by the check. Under the other forms the ratio of the round bound to the round's layer work, `(29 m + 3 d + 100)/((1 + m)(1 + d))`, is at most 33 for m, d >= 1, and `(3 d + 102)/(1 + d)` is at most 52.5 for m = 0 and d >= 1. The inner Results of the rounds are separate records bounded by their own rows. |
| `qhd/constrained_records.py::AugmentedLagrangianRecord` with nested `BoxRefinementResult` records | R saved rounds, m kept constraints, d variables, n_r completed levels and b_r split levels in round r | For solver-produced records with integer entropy, one-entry round spawn keys, at most one QHD missing-data reason per round and level spawn keys of length at most two, `R <= options.max_iterations`, `n_r <= refinement.max_levels` and `b_r <= min(n_r, refinement.max_splits)` separately in each round, with b_r = 0 when splits are disabled. The paragraph under this table gives the leaf bound. | Structurally proportional, with root and zero-level configuration terms explicit. Leaves include nulls and the content hashes of exported records, and exclude object keys and containers. The hash wrapper adds two scalar leaves to the hashed fields. Identity JSON bytes are unbudgeted because text lengths, including aggregated unknown-count reasons, and integer digit counts are not bounded by this formula or `qhd.max_bytes`. Inner QHD Results are separate records. |
| `qhd/refinement_records.py::BoxRefinementResult` | n completed levels, b split levels, d variables and the length g of both Gaussian parameter vectors, or g = 0 for other initial states | `options.max_levels` bounds n, and `b <= min(n, options.max_splits)`, with b = 0 when splits are disabled. At most `2*g + 147 + n*(14*d + 77) + b*(8*d + 13)` scalar leaves for level spawn keys of length at most two, or `2*g + 147 + n*(14*d + 76) + b*(8*d + 13)` for standalone refinement. The fixed part includes 88 leaves for total and stopped resources and `34 + 2*g` for QHD. The QHD contribution is 15 direct scalar settings, including `encoding` and `kinetic_model`, 3 content hashes, 5 schedule leaves, `4 + 2*g` initial-state leaves and 7 `BinarySynthesis` leaves. The binary-synthesis record is exported for both encodings. Each completed level counts at least d*K running-total work, where K is `qhd.num_grid_points`. For d >= 1 and K >= 2, `14*d + 77 <= 46*d*K` without a split and `22*d + 90 <= 56*d*K` with a split. | Structurally proportional, with the fixed configuration term present even at zero levels. Leaves include nulls and the content hashes of exported records, and exclude object keys and containers. The hash wrapper adds two scalar leaves to the hashed fields. Identity JSON bytes are unbudgeted because scalar strings and integers have variable encoded lengths. Live QHD Results are outside the record's content hash. |
| `qhd/records.py::SupportValues` and `QHDReconstruction.initial_amplitudes` | E total support entries, up to `D = K**d` for a full support, and d K one-variable grid entries | `QHD._admit_symbolic_work` with the byte formulas of `method._support_table_bytes` | Covered. QHD support tables and initial amplitudes are checked by `QHD._admit_symbolic_work` before their construction and first content-hash computation. Their content hashes read each array's digest in place, so the identity JSON grows with the number of tables and initial vectors and the decimal digits of their sizes. The byte formulas in `_support_table_bytes` count it beside the live arrays. |
| QHD grids and marginals, QLS and QSP polynomial coefficients and phases, LCHS quadrature and Duhamel nodes, Lanczos moments, FixedGCIM pencils, `PlanEstimate` and telemetry rows | Grid points, degree, nodes, Krylov dimension, basis size or assessments | Their checks: QHD selected operation sizes, the QLS and QSP polynomial and phase arrays compared with `max_bytes` before they are formed, the LCHS quadrature work and byte checks (for Duhamel nodes an explicit `max_quadrature_work` compared with `duhamel_nodes**2` in `src/nwqlib/algorithms/lchs/host.py`), the Krylov dimension, `max_basis_size=64` and the telemetry comparison's `max_comparisons=100000` | Fixed or method-level limits independent of D. |

**`ir/records.py::Program` and `SelectedConstruction`.** For b>1 basis states and G nonempty groups, sampled FixedGCIM with both the real and imaginary parts measured has b²G experiments, the larger of its two measurement choices, and real-only measurement has b(b+1)G/2. With both parts measured, two body/batch definitions per experiment, block and signature definitions and register/classical declarations give `(2*b**2+2)*G+4*b+19` combined declarations. At b=3 this is `20*G+31`. For a 20-qubit, 8083-term molecular Hamiltonian, G=1285 groups give 11,565 circuits and 25,731 declarations with both parts measured. Even singleton grouping of its Pauli terms has at most 161,691 declarations. This is a record count and sets aside no arrays. Planning, the count of kept fields, work, bytes and the work submitted for execution remain separate controls.

**Pauli-term reconstructions and readout specs.** `LanczosReconstruction` holds no per-term data. It saves the scalar frame and the content hash of its Pauli table, and the bound Plan derives its SELECT readout table (`src/nwqlib/algorithms/lanczos/readout.py::LanczosReadout`) from that table after planning and after loading. `S = 3J + 112` bytes with `J = max(2q + 120, 150)`, which is 562 bytes for q up to 15. Each record's identity JSON is computed on its own, a nested record first, so one copy holds a term in its widest single layout. That layout is J bytes: 150 for a per-term `PauliTerm` or `PauliCoefficient` record, which enters its parent as a 145-character reference, with a separator and 4 bytes of list brackets, and at most `2q + 120` for a label and coefficient written inline, as in the readout spec of a provider-estimate Expectation, QPE's kept labels, ADAPT's Hamiltonian and pool terms and the Lanczos rows. Three copies of J cover the two JSON copies made for the content hash and one copy that bounds the records' own label and coefficient data. Each per-term record also keeps its content hash, a 71-character string of 112 bytes on CPython 3.12, for as long as the Plan. A route that builds no per-term record, such as QPE or Lanczos, is charged the same share, because the input is checked before a Method is chosen. In an exact Expectation Plan with q = 6, 12 and 40 and 200 to 400 random Pauli labels with standard normal coefficients and an occupation reference state, the reconstruction's identity JSON grew by 145 bytes per term and the readout spec's by q + 3, and the content hashes that the per-term records kept added 112 bytes per term (Python 3.12.14 on macOS arm64). Quantum ADAPT stores each commutator as arrays. Its content hash reads their digests, and commutator construction counts each completed table against the byte limit (see the row for `_commutator_arrays`). At q = 12, FixedGCIM with two occupation basis states and LCHS on a periodic stencil with product-formula evolution add 8.9 to 12.8 KB per Pauli term of the observable or Hamiltonian, mostly in the Program and its selections. That growth far exceeds J, so `AdmissionLimits` (the `ir/records.py::Program` row) bounds them instead, as it bounds the group experiments of sampled Expectation. The largest accepted Plans of these kinds, for FixedGCIM and LCHS, hold 341 and 57 terms and 4.4 and 0.6 MB of JSON. QLS on `PeriodicStencil(12, mass=1.0, diffusion=0.25)` with the all-zero occupation RHS, and with the labels and coefficients of the sampled Expectation measurement in the paragraph above, binds each term of an exact `QuadraticForm` or `NormalizedExpectation` output once, in the parameters of its one reduction. Its Plan JSON grew by 38.5 bytes per term between the 40- and 80-term prefixes, within J. Sampled QLS Program size also depends on the distinct measured-register maps. Allocate, Release, coherent evolution and one-site basis changes are shared. Each distinct position/wire pair supplies one Measure definition, and each distinct measured register supplies one Sequence. Their additional record allowance and count-table bounds are stated in the QLS sampled count registers row. The Program itself is checked against its limits on kept fields and lifecycle work.

**`AugmentedLagrangianRecord` with nested `BoxRefinementResult` records.** Write H for the scalar leaves outside `iterations`, including the QHD configuration, root refinement options and aggregate resources, and g_r for the length of both Gaussian parameter vectors, or zero for other initial states. If H_other excludes the root QHD field as well, `H = H_other + 34 + 2*g_0`, where g_0 is the root Gaussian length or zero for other initial states. The scalar-leaf bound is `H + R*(6*m + 2*d + 91) + sum_r(2*g_r + 147 + n_r*(14*d + 77) + b_r*(8*d + 13))`, summing over nested refinements that exist. The direct round bound under `inequality_form="phr"` is `6*m + 2*d + 91`, including at most 49 resource leaves. Under the other forms each round has its own bound, at most `29*m + 3*d + 100` for m >= 1 or `3*d + 102` for m = 0, and the level terms of its refinement count the `d + s` variables of its inner problem with s slack variables ([augmented-Lagrangian record size](#augmented-lagrangian-record-size)). `_admit_layer_work` bounds the work set aside for the layer's evaluations. Each completed level's QHD running total includes at least d*K units, where K is `qhd.num_grid_points`. For d >= 1 and K >= 2, `14*d + 77 <= 46*d*K` without a split and `22*d + 90 <= 56*d*K` with a split.

Revisit when a record type starts holding data that grows with the problem, or when a per-term Pauli record grows past J.

##### Augmented-Lagrangian record size

Count the scalar JSON leaves of the exported `qhd/constrained_records.py::AugmentedLagrangianRecord`, each number, Boolean, string or null once, including every nested record's schema, parent and content hashes, and excluding object keys and containers. For solver-produced records with integer entropy, one-entry round spawn keys and at most one QHD missing-data reason per round, a round outside its nested refinement under `inequality_form="phr"` has at most `6 m + 2 d + 91` leaves for m kept constraints and d variables, the registered bound. Its `ALEvaluation` has at most `5 m + 2 d + 24`, its `mode_status` included, its `ALResources` at most 49 (18 counts, 14 nullable with one reason pair each, and 3 content hashes), and its other fields, a null refinement and a null representation included, at most `m + 18`. Under the other forms, a round's `InnerRepresentation` with s slack variables and T trials has at most `d + m + 10 s + 10 T + 12` leaves. Replacing its null placeholder adds at most `d + m + 10 s + 10 T + 11` leaves. The evaluation's slack fields add at most `2 max(s - 1, 0)` leaves, because both remain null when s = 0. The round therefore has at most `6 m + 2 d + 91 + d + m + 10 s + 10 T + 11 + 2 max(s - 1, 0)` leaves. With `s, T <= m`, this is at most `29 m + 3 d + 100` for m >= 1, and at most `3 d + 102` when m = 0.

A nested refinement with n completed levels and b split levels adds at most `2 g + 147 + n (14 d + 77) + b (8 d + 13)` ([refinement record size](#refinement-record-size)), with d replaced by the `d + s` variables of a round with s slack variables, where g is the common length of a `GaussianState`'s center and widths, or zero for other initial states, and level spawn keys have at most two entries. Let H count the leaves outside `iterations`. If H_other excludes the root `qhd` field as well, then `H = H_other + 34 + 2 g_0`, where g_0 is the root Gaussian length or zero for other initial states. H includes the problem, configurations, preprocessing, limits, scales, aggregate resources and remaining root fields. With R saved rounds, the bound is `H + R (6 m + 2 d + 91)`, or H plus the sum of the round bounds above under another form, plus the nested-refinement bounds above, summed over refinements that exist. Adding each full refinement to the round bound overcounts its null placeholder by one.

There are at most `options.max_iterations` rounds and each nested history has at most `refinement.max_levels` levels, with `b <= min(n, refinement.max_splits)` separately in each round and b = 0 when splits are disabled. An unsplit level has at most `14 d + 77` leaves and a split level at most `22 d + 90`. Each completed level's running total counts at least d K work units (NWQLib's count of planned classical operations), where K is `qhd.num_grid_points`. For d >= 1 and K >= 2, these level bounds are at most `46 d K` and `56 d K`, respectively. Root fields and each refinement's fixed term `2 g + 147` are accounted for separately, including zero-level refinements. These counts give no byte allowance. Text such as the joined unknown-count reasons and integer digit counts have no fixed length, so content-hash JSON bytes are not budgeted by this rule or `qhd.max_bytes`.

##### Refinement record size

Count the scalar JSON leaves of the exported `qhd/refinement_records.py::BoxRefinementResult`, each number, Boolean, string or null once, including every nested record's schema, parent and content hashes and every allowed unknown-count reason pair, and excluding object keys and containers. An unsplit level has at most `14 d + 75 + s` leaves for d variables and s entries in its spawn key. Its dimension-dependent fields contribute `14 d` from `box` and `next_box` (2d each), `intervals` (2d), the center and widths of a best-point `initial_state` (d each), and `spacing`, `point_indices`, `point`, `unit_point`, `axis_masses` and `region_axis_counts` (d each). Its 24 other scalar fields include `region_count`, `mode_status`, the null `split` and scalar `split_declined`, and `initial_state` adds at most its kind and 3 content hashes. There are 3 content-hash leaves and at most 44 resource leaves, from 15 counts, 3 resource content hashes and at most 13 reason pairs. A `StallSplit` has `8 d + 14` leaves, replacing one null and adding `8 d + 13`. Standalone refinement has s = 1 and the augmented-Lagrangian layer s = 2, so the unsplit bounds are `14 d + 76` and `14 d + 77`, and the split bounds are `22 d + 89` and `22 d + 90`, respectively.

The two region count fields, `region_axis_counts` and `region_count`, contribute d + 1 scalar leaves for counts. Exact readout (`shots=None`, in which a simulator computes probabilities, Pauli expectation values or amplitudes without shot sampling, subject to numerical rounding) uses two nulls, which also fit this bound for d >= 1. The separately computed report dictionary is outside the exported record and its content hash.

The fields outside `levels` add at most `2 g + 147` leaves. This counts 11 for the eight scalar result fields and three content hashes, 14 for the options, 88 for total and stopped resources, and `34 + 2 g` for QHD. The QHD term includes 15 direct scalar settings, including `encoding` and `kinetic_model`, 3 content hashes, 5 schedule leaves, `4 + 2 g` initial-state leaves and 7 `BinarySynthesis` leaves. The binary-synthesis record has 4 settings and 3 content hashes and is exported for both encodings. Here g is the common length of a `GaussianState`'s center and widths, or zero for other initial states. With n completed levels, b split levels and spawn keys of length at most two, the bound is `2 g + 147 + n (14 d + 77) + b (8 d + 13)`. Use 76 in place of 77 for standalone refinement. `options.max_levels` bounds n, and `b <= min(n, options.max_splits)`, with b = 0 when splits are disabled. Each completed level counts at least d K running-total work, where K is `qhd.num_grid_points`. For d >= 1 and K >= 2, `14 d + 77 <= 46 d K` without a split and `22 d + 90 <= 56 d K` with a split, since `46 d K - 14 d >= 78 d >= 77` and `56 d K - 22 d >= 90 d >= 90`. The fixed fields remain when no level completes. These counts give no byte allowance because text lengths and integer digit counts vary. Identity JSON bytes are unbudgeted, and live QHD results are outside the record's content hash.

## Backends

These defaults and size limits belong to the backend adapters and the provider services they call.

### Provider and compiler policies

| Constant | Value | Site | Reason and revisit condition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `execution.py::LOCAL_SIMULATORS` | `{"qiskit_aer", "nwqsim"}` | `src/nwqlib/execution.py` | The backend kinds that simulate on this machine, local Aer and a locally launched NWQ-Sim runner. On them and on classical-routine execution, a classical computation run in place of a circuit, `prepare(plan, settings="all")` prepares every setting known before running, because their preparation is local circuit construction with no remote compilation or provider charge. A Run on any other kind fills its unset circuit and shot budgets from `PAID_BUDGETS`. Revisit condition: A new local simulator, or a decision on preparing every setting on remote backends ([Limitations and open work](ROADMAP.md#preparing-every-setting-on-remote-backends)). |
| `backends/nwqsim.py`, `slurm.py`, `ionq.py`, `ibm_runtime.py` and `nexus.py`, `optimization_level` defaults | 0, 0, 0, 1 and 1 | `src/nwqlib/backends/nwqsim.py`, `src/nwqlib/backends/slurm.py`, `src/nwqlib/backends/ionq.py`, `src/nwqlib/backends/ibm_runtime.py`, `src/nwqlib/backends/nexus.py` | The compiler optimization levels whose preparation records (the records that a backend writes about each prepared circuit) carry no `optimization_level` exclusion. A user may choose any level from 0 to 3, except with IonQ's `"native"` gateset, which accepts only level 0. Another level adds that exclusion. A preparation record carrying it has no `state_error()` bound, and the code that uses it takes its documented fallback. No error or resource accounting of the optimization is attempted. Revisit condition: An error derivation that covers an optimized compilation. |

| `backends/ionq.py`, shots | 1–1000000 | `src/nwqlib/backends/ionq.py` | The `shots` range of the single-circuit and multi-circuit job types in IonQ's v0.4 [create-job reference](https://docs.ionq.com/api-reference/v0.4/jobs/create-job), which gives minimum 1 and maximum 1,000,000. A particular device may accept fewer shots. Revisit condition: A changed API version or a device-specific limit. |

JSON text and outgoing UTF-8 bytes coexist. The factor is not a proof of Python-object or requests/SDK peak memory. Revisit condition: A changed serializer or transport lifetime. |
| `backends/ionq.py::_request` | read size 64 KiB and parse allowance `5 * size` | `src/nwqlib/backends/ionq.py` | Untuned read size. With `size` bytes already read, each read asks for `min(64 KiB, max_response_bytes + 1 - size)` bytes, so the reader holds at most `max_response_bytes + 1` body bytes, and the extra byte reveals an oversized body. This relies on urllib3 2, which the `ionq` extra requires, returning at most the requested number of decoded bytes per read. Five times the bytes read is an untuned allowance for the parsed JSON, checked against the Run's data cap before `json.loads`. It is not a measured object-size ratio. Revisit condition: Measured parse memory or a changed response format. |

It has at most `min(s, 2**q)` entries, each a quoted state key of at most q characters (the binary v2 key, no shorter than the decimal v1 key), a colon, a count of at most `len(str(s))` digits and a comma. The 44 bytes (`_HISTOGRAM_FRAME`) are the fixed v2 structure `{"histogram":{"registers":{"output_all":{...}}}}` less the last entry's comma, the larger of the two formats that `_decode` reads (v1 adds one byte). Preparation refuses a circuit above `max_response_bytes` before any request. Whitespace and other response fields are counted only when the body is read. Revisit condition: A changed histogram format or key spelling. |

Revisit condition: A changed item naming or association record. |
| `backends/ionq.py::_decode` | `m*(c+64)` bytes for a histogram of m entries and c classical bits | `src/nwqlib/backends/ionq.py` | A coarse check of the decode before it starts, covering the larger of its two routes. Up to 64 qubits and 64 classical bits, NumPy 2.5.2's unique with inverse indices, which merges equal outcomes, holds about seven eight-byte arrays and one Boolean mask per entry, about 57 bytes. Wider inputs use Python integers and bit-string keys, each written as JSON in c characters, at most seven count digits and its punctuation, c+16 bytes. It does not bound Python dictionary, string or integer memory. Revisit condition: A changed count domain, key spelling, output representation or unique implementation. |
| `backends/nexus.py`, shots per program | 10000 | `src/nwqlib/backends/nexus.py` | H2's published job limit keeps a job within its allowed calibration interval. Revisit condition: A changed provider contract or target. |
| `backends/nexus.py::_download_bytes` and `_decode_bytes` | `shots * (len(bits) + 8)` per download, and `shots * (4 * width + 8)` | `src/nwqlib/backends/nexus.py` | Untuned allowances for one downloaded pytket result and for the outcome tuples and count labels built from it. At download, `refresh` uses the largest item because the program that a result belongs to is known only after download. Revisit condition: Measured result sizes or a changed result format. |

The allowance covers a ten-digit supported job ID, the separator, the known cluster and one LF in canonical sbatch parsable output. The normal response cap and the check against the Run's data limit still apply to every command. Status and accounting responses can require a larger cap. Revisit condition: A changed job-ID domain, cluster-name rule or sbatch acknowledgement form, sbatch output on stderr, or a transport that introduces CRLF. |

The byte caps and deadlines are enforced independently of these values. Revisit condition: A measured latency or throughput need. |
| `backends/nwqec.py`, epsilon | `1e-10` per gate or `1e-2` otherwise | `src/nwqlib/backends/nwqec.py` | Untuned requested synthesis precision defaults under distinct compiler policies. NWQLib counts one gate per entry of each circuit's IonQ JSON gate list, and the reference does not define the count further. Decomposition stops earlier once only instruction classes, names and representations that Aer supports remain. The metadata counts the passes made, including the final check. Some explicit-analysis preparation paths still use the fixed budget. Revisit when Qiskit/Aer instruction support changes. |

This is separate from SHOTS measurement budgets and the resource `shots` field. |
| Aer job identifier length | 36 ASCII characters | `src/nwqlib/backends/connection.py::AerBackend.native_job_id_length` | Aer 0.18.0 generates each job identifier with `str(uuid.uuid4())`. Its JSON string costs 38 bytes. The bytes set aside for each probability point of a trajectory count the array records with a job identifier of this length (`src/nwqlib/_prepared_execution.py::_probability_point_metadata`). Revisit when the job format changes. |
| Aer statevector thread cap | `max(os.cpu_count(), OMP_NUM_THREADS)` | `src/nwqlib/backends/qiskit_aer.py::_aer_state_threads` | Positive cap passed to Aer as `max_parallel_threads`, resolved once per open Run, recorded in the preparation metadata and reapplied on restoration. The trajectory memory check uses the same value for the transient per-worker probability and index arrays of a general marginal save. Aer takes the minimum of this cap and its automatic OpenMP maximum, so the cap can reduce parallelism.
