### QHD rotation law

These formulas describe the chosen synthesis model and are separate from measured compiler output, like the shared [circuit-free synthesis laws](#circuit-free-synthesis-laws). Revisit them when the construction or compiler changes, using independent matched-workload evidence.

The one-hot QHD rotation formula (`src/nwqlib/algorithms/qhd/resources.py::rotation_population`) counts the rotations of Qiskit 2.6.0's definitions of the emitted gates. Up to four controls `MCPhaseGate` applies MCRZ gates, whose fixed parts are the 7 T gates of each Toffoli of `synth_mcx_n_dirty_i15` (`_mcx_rotations`). From five controls it is `synth_mcp_noaux_sp22`, whose controlled RX gates add `8 s - 20` exact T gates and `4 ((s - 3)**2 - 1)` arbitrary rotations at fixed angles from pi/8 down to `pi/2**(s - 2)` on s qubits (`_projector_slots`). The formula and its test `test_projector_rotations_match_the_qiskit_definitions` in `tests/test_qhd_resources.py` compare these with the expanded definitions. Revisit when that test fails after a Qiskit upgrade. `T_PER_PRECISION_BIT = 3` in the same module is the leading coefficient of the typical Ross–Selinger T count `3 log2(1/eps)` of one Rz synthesized to operator error eps (arXiv:1403.2975v3), used with intercept zero because neither that result nor NWQEC 0.1.2 supports a finite intercept. The resulting T estimate is labeled `estimate`. On one small QHD circuit it was 392.9 against 348 T and T-inverse gates compiled by NWQEC 0.1.2 at the same budget (docs/algorithms/qhd.md, "Fault-tolerant resources", measured with Python 3.12.14 and Qiskit 2.5.2 on macOS arm64). Revisit with a qualified synthesis formula or a calibration that the library uses.

## Subroutines

These constants belong to the shared subroutines: exact dense synthesis, QSP, product formulas, block encodings, state preparation, the projected eigensolver and the linear-algebra work formulas.

### Circuit-free synthesis laws

The following formulas describe the chosen synthesis model. They are separate from measured compiler output. Revisit them when the construction or compiler changes, using independent matched-workload evidence.

The classical cost of one exact synthesis `_dense_synthesis.dense_unitary_circuit` on m qubits, with M = 2**m, is given by `_dense_synthesis.dense_synthesis_size` as `113 M**3/4 + (m**2 + 16 m + 512) M**2` work units (NWQLib's count of planned classical operations), `320 M**2 + 65536` working bytes and `352 M**2 + 16384` kept bytes. The units are those of the dense-completion work in the row "Dense block-encoding arrays and work", h**3 for a product of h-square matrices and 8 h**3 for an SVD or Schur factorization. A generic block-ZXZ step on an s-square block costs 113 (s/2)**3 units and passes four half-size blocks on, so the recursion stays below (113/4) M**3. Each of at most M**2/16 two-qubit blocks is allowed 8192 units for its A.2 work, which stays below 7200 units also with the blocks that a failed batch of `_dense_synthesis._apply_a2` discards, and the elementwise passes stay below (m**2 + 16 m) M**2. The kept-byte formula allows 256 bytes for each of at most 11 M**2/8 instructions. Kept circuits, rebuilt from their gate lists in a fresh process, held 124 to 173 bytes of resident memory per instruction for m = 3 to 9, and the kept-byte formula was 1.7 to 5.6 times that memory for m = 2 to 9 and 2.0 and 2.2 times at m = 8 and 9. The traced working peak stayed at or below 0.54 of its formula for m = 1 to 9, and 0.45 for m = 6 to 9, with Haar-random, near-identity and block-diagonal inputs. A Haar-random input took 0.12 s at m = 7, 0.57 s at m = 8 and 2.6 s at m = 9 on an Apple-silicon Mac with one thread, 0.65 to 1.1 s per 1e9 units at m = 8 and 9. `_dense_synthesis._A2_BATCH = 32` is the largest number of consecutive two-qubit blocks whose Weyl frames the A.2 loop computes in one stacked call. It keeps the arrays of one batch near 100 KB, and without a cap the synthesis at m = 9 took the same time. `qiskit_compat.dense_synthesis_widths` lists the syntheses that a rewrite would make, without making them. QLS counts the forward and the adjoint synthesis of a controlled dense query, which a Run builds once each, against `max_work` and `max_bytes` at planning (`src/nwqlib/algorithms/qls/host_planning.py::_admit_query_synthesis`). LCHS counts one synthesis per physical `dense_exact` branch with address bits and at least two system qubits, and the syntheses of a dense-dilation QSP child (`src/nwqlib/algorithms/lchs/parameters.py::_dense_child_syntheses`), against `max_select_work` and `max_bytes`. The QSP evolution and joint-generator builders check their own `max_work` and `max_bytes`, the MPS builder checks its at most `layers * (n - 1)` two-qubit syntheses against its `max_svd_work` and `max_bytes`, and circuit construction inside a Run and a backend that translates a circuit to its gate basis check theirs against the physical memory before they start and count them in `ExecutionTrace.synthesis_work`. Revisit when `_qsd` or `_apply_a2` changes its steps or when Qiskit changes how a circuit stores standard gates.

`_dense_synthesis.AUTO_WHOLE_MATRIX_MAX_CONTROLS = 1`, called K, is the largest number of controls for which the `"auto"` control route (`DenseExact.control_route` of LCHS, `dense_control_route` elsewhere) synthesizes the whole controlled matrix (`controlled_unitary_circuit`) instead of controlling each gate of the synthesized unitary ([Exact dense synthesis](development/dense_synthesis.md#dense-control-route)). For an M-square unitary with k controls the whole-matrix synthesis costs about `11 (2**k M)**3` work units by `controlled_synthesis_size`, about 88 M**3 with one control and 704 M**3 with two, against about 28 M**3 for `dense_synthesis_size` alone, and its bytes grow as `(2**k M)**2`, four times per added control. For Haar-random unitaries on 2 to 5 qubits (Qiskit 2.5.2) it took 3.2 to 4.0 times fewer CX than the gate-wise route with one control, 3.6 to 3.8 times fewer with two and 1.9 to 2.1 times fewer with three. With four controls the gate-wise route took fewer CX, 15.5 to 19.0 percent fewer with the `random_unitary` seeds 10 n + 4 for n = 2 to 5 qubits and 15.5 to 23.9 percent over the seeds 10 n + 4, 10 n + 104 and 10 n + 204, counted after `transpile` at optimization level 0 with its default `qubits_initially_zero=True`. With one or two controls it was also faster to build and transpile to CX and U gates on 3 to 5 qubits. K is chosen on the classical cost that the checks count. With one control the whole-matrix work is of the same order as the synthesis alone for a CX saving of 3 to 4 times. With two controls the saving stays near 3.7 times, but the work is about 704 M**3, so a controlled QLS query of a dense dilation of 64 padded coordinates would need about 3.2e9 units, above an explicit `max_work=1_000_000_000`, where the gate-wise route needs 2.4e8. With two controls the formulas also give the whole-matrix synthesis `256 (4 M)**2` working and `176 (4 M)**2` kept bytes, against `320 M**2` and `352 M**2` for the synthesis alone, about 12.8 and 8 times as many for large M (12.65 and 7.98 times at M = 128). Each further control multiplies the whole-matrix work by 8 and its bytes by 4, and the saving falls to about 2 times with three. `"whole_matrix"` is available for any control count, and the construction limits count its cost (`controlled_synthesis_size`). Revisit when `controlled_unitary_circuit` changes its cost or CX count, when Qiskit's control of a gate changes its counts (`CONTROLLED_U_INSTRUCTIONS`), or when a workload with two controls needs the CX saving.

On the gate-wise route, when a construction controls a composite gate that holds exact syntheses, Qiskit's `add_control` unrolls the composite and controls every synthesized gate, once for each place where the synthesis occurs (`qiskit_compat.dense_control_counts`). This control step has its own formula, the gate-wise control formula of `_dense_synthesis.gatewise_control_counts` and `gatewise_control_size`. For one synthesis on m qubits with k controls, `gatewise_control_counts` bounds the gates that Qiskit unrolls (the gate count of `dense_synthesis_gate_census` plus the global phase) and the instructions that it emits. `CONTROLLED_U_INSTRUCTIONS` and `CONTROLLED_RZ_INSTRUCTIONS` hold the number of instructions that Qiskit 2.6.0 emits for a U and an RZ gate with 1 to 64 controls, measured because its dirty-ancilla X synthesis has no published closed-form size. A CX and the global phase emit one instruction and an H seven. `gatewise_control_size` counts `2048 gates + 16 instructions` work units, `1024 gates + 320 instructions + 65536` working bytes and `320 instructions + 16384` kept bytes. The control step does no floating-point linear algebra, so its units were calibrated by time. Each unrolled gate costs one Python call, 6 to 16 us with one control, and a U gate took up to 320 us with up to eight controls (Apple-silicon Mac, one thread, Qiskit 2.5.2). For Haar-random syntheses on 4 to 7 qubits with one to eight controls, Qiskit's control call took 4.4 to 35 ns per unit, and the synthesis took 1.6 to 15 ns per unit of its own formula. Both formulas feed the same limits, which count work units, not time. Per unit, the control call took 0.45 to 3.3 times as long as the synthesis on 4 qubits and 2.6 to 21 times as long on 7 qubits. With these units an explicit QLS `max_work=1_000_000_000` allows a controlled dense dilation of 64 padded coordinates. An LCHS `dense_exact` SELECT of 16 branches on 5 system qubits needs 7.1e7 units for its control steps, 2.5e7 for its syntheses and 8.2e6 for its branch exponentials in the test problem `_dense_select_problem` of `tests/test_lchs_resource_structural_law.py`, 1.04e8 in total, which fits an explicit billion-unit SELECT work cap. For generic homogeneous branches, that work cap and the address-slot limit allow up to 64 power-of-two branches at five system qubits. These are SELECT-stage projections, independent of spectral and PREP checks. In circuits of one instruction kind a parameterless standard gate held 40 to 59 bytes of resident memory, a gate with angles 123 to 177, an MCX gate 355 and an MCPhase gate 733. For 4 to 7 qubits and one to eight controls, kept controlled gates held 67 to 267 bytes per emitted instruction, below the coarse 320 bytes per instruction of the byte formulas. `test_gatewise_control_tables_match_installed_qiskit` in `tests/test_synthesis_admission.py` recomputes the tabulated counts. Revisit when those tests fail after a Qiskit upgrade, when `qiskit_compat.controlled` changes its route, or when a workload needs a control step that these units count far below its time.

`MCX_CX_BY_CONTROLS` in `src/nwqlib/subroutines/_mcx_counts.py` gives the CX count of an X gate with `1 <= k <= 64` valued controls. Entry k is the CX count of NWQLib's `controlled(XGate(), k)` after Qiskit 2.6.0 transpiles it without ancillas to basis `cx,u` at optimization level 0, which is the logical circuit before routing. Open and closed control states give the same count. The counts are 6, 14, 36 and 82 CX at k = 2, 3, 4 and 5, 362 at k = 10 and 2402 at k = 25, so no per-control constant describes them. Up to 32 controls Qiskit synthesizes the gate with `synth_mcx_noaux_sp22`, which uses Qiskit's dedicated CX, Toffoli, C3X and C4X circuits up to four controls and the construction of da Silva and Park (Phys. Rev. A 106, 042602, doi:10.1103/PhysRevA.106.042602) from five, with `4k**2 - 4k + 2` CX. Above 32 controls it uses `synth_mcx_noaux_hp24`, which has no published closed-form CX count, so the table holds the values. The module imports nothing, so the circuit-free formulas of QLS and LCHS read the table without importing Qiskit. Every count read from it is recorded as an estimate, because another Qiskit version can synthesize the gate differently. A gate with more than 64 controls has no CX formula. In QLS, `_primitive_cx_law` in `src/nwqlib/algorithms/qls/quantum.py` prices each X gate with `2 <= k <= 64` controls in the Program, such as the projector flips and the `Q_b'` and `A_t` predicates. A singly controlled X is exactly one CX. Other controlled primitives and controlled queries have no CX formula, so only the Hermitian inverse route can reach a concrete CX total, and there the table enters through the projector flips of an encoding with at least two block ancillas. The shortcut circuits and the dilation of a non-Hermitian inverse report an unknown total. The LCHS SELECT formulas read the table for the multi-controlled X gates that Qiskit adds when it controls a branch (`native._controlled_gate_cx`), and the QSP route counts read it for the projector toggles, walk reflections and lift gates of their emitters (`qsp/evolution._shared_gate_cx`, `qsp/evolution._qubitized_gate_cx`). `test_multi_controlled_x_cx_table_matches_installed_qiskit_synthesis` in `tests/test_mcx_counts.py` recomputes every entry with the installed Qiskit. Revisit when that test fails after a Qiskit upgrade, or when the gates move to an ancilla-assisted construction with a closed-form count.

The gate-slot bound of the MPS outer PREP is `3 * layers * max(0, n - 1)` CX. In `mps_to_circuit`, every layer truncates to rank at most two and the last site has right bond one, so at most n-1 local two-qubit unitaries are emitted. `_dense_synthesis.dense_unitary_circuit` synthesizes each exactly to rounding with at most three CX. Initial preparation and LCU preparation have separate provider/layer inputs, and the latter has multiplicity two. This circuit-free bound describes the chosen construction before hardware routing, and early rank-one termination can reduce the cost of the built circuit.

### Dense synthesis rounding window

`ROUNDING_WINDOW = 2**-46` in `src/nwqlib/subroutines/_dense_synthesis.py` is the largest deviation that the exact dense synthesis of controlled gates, of the translation to a backend's gate basis and of MPS preparation treats as zero or as an exact special value: a Weyl coordinate from a multiple of pi/4, a one-qubit rotation from the identity, an off-diagonal block entry from zero, or an entry of a block's deviation from a multiple of the identity from zero. A replaced coordinate changes the operator by at most this amount in operator norm, an omitted one-qubit gate by at most 1.5 times this amount, and a dropped block entry changes one entry by at most this amount. The value is 128 unit roundoffs (u = 2**-53). For exactly special inputs these quantities come out of the factorizations at about 1e-16, so the window recognizes them, and a deliberate deviation such as a Weyl coordinate of 1e-12 is far outside it and keeps its gates. A larger window would drop real rotations. A smaller one would only cost CX for special inputs, not accuracy. For `U = expm(-i t G)` with random Hermitian G, t from 1e-10 to 10 and one to five qubits, the largest entry error measured was 14 u times the dimension, and `tests/test_dense_synthesis.py` checks 64 u times the dimension. Revisit when the synthesis changes or a workload needs special inputs recognized at a coarser precision.

### QSP phase synthesis and evolution targets

Each definition includes a rationale comment.

| Constant | Value | Site | Reason and revisit condition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `QSP_SOLVER_RESIDUAL_TOLERANCE` | 1e-12 | `src/nwqlib/subroutines/qsp/phases.py` | Default acceptance threshold for the coefficient one-norm of the polynomial rebuilt from the synthesized phases, in the normalized target frame. It is a solver control, not a total Hamiltonian-evolution error bound. Revisit for a different floating-point precision. |
| `QSP_EVOLUTION_TARGET_CAP` (`evolution.py`) | 0.999 | `src/nwqlib/subroutines/qsp/evolution.py` | Common circle cap of the cosine and sine targets of `prepare_qsp_evolution`, fixed before either parity solve. The exact cosine reaches \|f\| = 1 at x = 0, and the exact sine does for tau >= pi/2, while their finite truncations need not stay at or below one. Canonical synthesis needs a cap strictly below one, its ideal discretization bounds grow as the cap approaches one, and a lower cap costs amplitude in the oblivious amplitude amplification. LCHS `QSPBlockEncoding(route="auto")` compares this cap with 0.99, 0.9375 and 0.5 by the CX count of each route before any phase is synthesized. Revisit when the shared real QSVT target or its phase synthesis changes. |
| Chebyshev norming grid (`phases.py`) | `64(d+1)` Chebyshev-zero nodes on [-1,1] | `src/nwqlib/subroutines/qsp/phases.py` | The shared code of the target cap and of the QLS and LCHS rescales uses Ehlich-Zeller Satz 2 (doi:10.1007/BF01111276), Eqs. (12)–(14), with factor `sec(d pi/(2N))`. At this density its inflation is at most `sec(pi/128)-1 ~= 3.01e-4`, below one third of the headroom 1e-3 that the default `QSP_EVOLUTION_TARGET_CAP` leaves. The implementation evaluates the exact-arithmetic inequality in binary64. |
| QSP preparation defaults (`evolution.py::prepare_qsp_evolution`, `phases.py::solve_wx_qsp_phases`, `phases.py::solve_laurent_qsp_phases`) | `residual_tolerance` 1e-12, `max_fft` None and `max_bytes` None (the physical memory) | `src/nwqlib/subroutines/qsp/evolution.py`, `src/nwqlib/subroutines/qsp/phases.py` | Explicit controls. `build_qsp_evolution_encoding` takes the same `residual_tolerance`, and LCHS derives its per-parity target ([Shared-route phase target](algorithms/lchs.md#shared-route-phase-target)). The transform length doubles until the reconstructed residual meets the tolerance. It stops with an error after a miss at a length that an ideal-arithmetic bound calls sufficient, at an explicit `max_fft`, or when the arrays of the next length would exceed `max_bytes`. No default caps the polynomial degree or the transform length. `max_bytes` bounds the dominant arrays of each attempt and of the Jacobi–Anger Bessel table before they are formed, and `max_fft` is an optional cap on the transform length. Bytes bound known simultaneous arrays rather than process RSS or vendor workspace. |

`python docs/scripts/qsp_phase_solver_sweep.py --evolution` runs `prepare_qsp_evolution` with its default controls at each point of a `tau` x `epsilon` grid, and each preparation synthesizes both parity targets. On the default grid, `tau = 0.05, 0.10, ..., 10` at `epsilon` 1e-2, 1e-3, 1e-4, 1e-6 and 1e-9, all 1000 preparations succeed at the cap 0.999, with degree at most 26, coefficient residual at most 9.99e-13 and at most 15 transform attempts for the two parities together. The sweep took 3.6 s (Python 3.12.14, NumPy 2.5.2 and SciPy 1.18.1 on an Apple M3 Max).

### Trotter error bounds and Pauli commutation tables {#other-numerical-guards}

| Constant | Value | Site | Reason and revisit condition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Trotter-bound imaginary coefficient window | 1e-12 absolute | `src/nwqlib/subroutines/trotterization/error_budget.py::_validated_terms` | Same window as the Qiskit Pauli-rotation path in `src/nwqlib/subroutines/hamiltonian_evolution/pauli_evolution.py`. It accepts the residues near 1e-17j that library constructions leave on exactly Hermitian operators and rejects genuinely complex coefficients. It is absolute, not relative to the coefficient scale. Revisit for operators whose coefficients are far from unit scale. |
| `EXACT_CENSUS_WORD_TESTS` | 1,300,000,000 logical nested word tests | `src/nwqlib/subroutines/trotterization/error_budget.py::choose_variant`, `census_structure`, used by `_bound_coefficient_evaluation`, `src/nwqlib/algorithms/qpe/powers.py::_census` and `src/nwqlib/algorithms/lchs/time_independent_terms.py::_node_bound_coefficients` | Selection threshold of the second-order Pauli-triangle expression, never a rejection limit. For p ordered kept terms on q qubits, with w = ceil(q/64) mask words and e_i the anticommuting partners j > i of term i, the full expression tests every k > i, k = j included, for each anticommuting pair, so it makes w*N(A) word tests with N(A) = sum_i e_i (p-i-1). The pair scan gives this count before any nested test. `bound_variant="auto"` takes the full expression when `N(A)*max(w, K)` is at or below the threshold and the degree-capped relaxation above it, where K is the number of weightings contracted with the same nested masks, the distinct k-nodes of an LCHS table and one elsewhere. K weightings make `K*N(A)` weight products beside the `w*N(A)` word tests, so the count keeps the nested work `w*N(A) + K*N(A)` within 2,600,000,000, the work that one weighting at w = 1 is allowed. Counting a weight product like a word test is a modeling choice without a measured cost ratio. At w = 1 and N(A) = 80,918,050, the 1000-term table below, 16 weightings take the full expression and 17 the relaxation. At second order an explicit variant is kept, and first order has only pair terms, recorded as `"exact_census"`. The count does not depend on memory, time, machine or byte limits, and a limit that the chosen expression exceeds refuses it with the requirement. LCHS counts the shared union structure of its k-nodes once, because the nested masks are formed once and reused by every node contraction, and all nodes of one table take the same expression. Workload: the threshold sits just above the 1,280,549,200 word tests of a 2500-term table. An earlier implementation of the full expression with a stored table of term triples took 0.41 s, 1.56 s and 7.5 s for p = 1000, 1500 and 2500 on random 20-qubit Pauli sums of 2- to 4-local terms, with 80,918,050, 270,132,409 and 1,280,549,200 word tests, on a machine that was not recorded. The counts were replayed from the saved input generators. The timings come from that implementation, not from this one. A random 400-term, 12-qubit Hamiltonian of 2- to 4-local terms for QCELS makes 7,286,537 word tests and uses the full expression with 630 common steps, both under the default memory limit and under `max_bytes=50_000_000`, where a memory-based choice of the relaxation gave 870. Revisit when a measured census of this implementation departs materially from that scale, or when the census kernel changes. |
| `_PAIR_TILE` | 32 pairs | `src/nwqlib/subroutines/trotterization/error_budget.py::coefficient_up` | The full expression forms the nested masks of 32 anticommuting pairs of one row at a time against all later terms, at most `40*32*p*w` bytes in `census_bytes`, which charges the largest tile that the pair rows can form, and contracts them with the magnitudes of every node. The tile sets the scratch size only. Each nested mass is one sum over the later terms, each row of pairs one reduction and the coefficient one reduction over the rows, so the value does not depend on the tile. An engineering choice. Revisit when the mask kernel changes. |
| Pauli commutation table work and bytes | Work `G + K*V`: `G = w*P`, `V = E` at order one, `G = w*P`, `V = 2p+4E` for the relaxation and `G = w*(P+N)`, `V = E+N` for the full expression, with `P = p(p-1)/2`, E anticommuting pairs, `N = N(A)` and K node weightings. Bytes `80p+16pw+3pq+8E+128p+64Kp`, plus `16Kp` for the relaxation or `40*T*w` for the full expression, with `T = max_i min(32, e_i)(p-i-1)` mask entries in the largest nested tile, zero without anticommuting pairs | `src/nwqlib/subroutines/trotterization/error_budget.py::census_work`, `census_bytes`, `pair_rows` | The work counts word visits of the mask tests and one visit per magnitude contribution. It is a proxy for explicit work limits, not a timing. The bytes estimate the dominant arrays: the census inputs, the masks, the label codes and two temporaries that the mask packing holds, the pair rows and their array headers, the scaled magnitudes and per-row partial sums of K weightings, the suffix and degree vectors of the relaxation and one tile of nested masks. In 45 cases with p from 120 to 800, q from 7 to 70 and K from 1 to 64, random tables and tables of commuting IZ labels with no anticommuting pair, the estimate was 1.10 to 2.19 times the traced allocation peak of the census and its K contractions (tracemalloc, Python 3.12.14 and NumPy 2.5.2 on an Apple M3 Max). It is not process RSS. `pair_rows` checks its rows against the limit as they grow. Revisit when the census kernel or its arrays change. |
| Pauli commutation table check of the public Trotter helpers | `bound_variant="auto"`, `max_work=None` (no limit) and `max_bytes=None` (the physical memory). Label conversion: `128*p*(q+1)` bytes, checked against `max_bytes` before conversion, and `16*p*(q+1)` logical preparation work, plus `p*q` label visits for packing | `src/nwqlib/subroutines/trotterization/error_budget.py::_bound_coefficient_evaluation`, used by `trotter_bound_coefficient`, `evaluate_trotter_bound` and `select_trotter_step_count` | Explicit limits only. The conversion bound covers the rendered-label list, source tuples and complex scalars, and validated tuples and real scalars. With `max_work`, the conversion and the `w*P` pair tests are checked before the labels are converted, and the conversion plus the work of the chosen expression after the pair scan and before any nested test. A refusal of the full second-order expression, chosen or requested, also states the work or bytes of the relaxation, which the caller can request. Revisit when the label conversion or the census kernel changes. |
| Trotter dense-comparison tolerance | 1e-12 relative | `tests/test_trotterization.py::_DENSE_TAIL_SUM_REL_TOL` | The anchor and seeded tests compare the Pauli-triangle coefficient W_up, an outward upper bound on the exact rational W for either bound variant, with the dense commutator sums of the same Hamiltonian. For these inputs the dense sums' magnitude bound B is below 5 W and their evaluation errs by at most gamma_256 B, so a relative allowance below 1.43e-13 covers the roundoff of both sides and of the comparison, derived in the comment at the constant. The allowance stays eleven orders of magnitude below the 13.64 % to 50 % decrease of W that a lost factor causes on the tight two-qubit anchor. Revisit when the fixtures, their generation or the dense helpers change. |
| `PAULI_COEFFICIENT_RTOL` | 1e-12 | `src/nwqlib/subroutines/pauli_decomposition.py` | Scale-covariant pruning of matrix-decomposition roundoff. The removed coefficient mass is propagated and deducted from product-formula error budgets. It also sets the LCHS node cutoff, over all labels with the identity included, so a large identity beside a small term can remove that term. A fixed-step run reports the removed mass as `trotter_pruning` in the frame of the physical solution. That value is one component and enters no total. |
| `hamiltonian_evolution/pauli_evolution.py::_matched_xx_yy` | imaginary window `1e-12` | `src/nwqlib/subroutines/hamiltonian_evolution/pauli_evolution.py` | Absolute window, in the coefficient's units, below which the imaginary part of an XX or YY coefficient is discarded before the pair uses one `XXPlusYYGate`. QHD kinetic coefficients are real. A larger imaginary part sends the block to the general Pauli-evolution path. Revisit condition: A relative window or another precision. |

### Block encoding, LCU and multiplexors

| Constant | Value | Site | Reason and revisit condition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Dense-dilation normalization relative slack | 1e-12 times alpha | `src/nwqlib/subroutines/block_encoding/core.py::_DENSE_ALPHA_RELATIVE_SLACK` | Compare a planned alpha with a computed value that is at most the spectral norm in exact arithmetic, the largest entry magnitude or the largest singular value of the already-required SVD, at the operator's scale. Planning, the explicit dense-dilation plan and the constructor use this one rule (`_require_alpha_covers`). LAPACK's SVD is backward stable, so a computed norm, and an alpha taken from it, can fall a few units in the last place below an entry magnitude or the exact norm. 1e-12 is about 9000 unit roundoffs, which covers that error at the dimensions a dense dilation serves, while a larger deficit is rejected with both raw values in the message. Revisit for a different precision or SVD error model. This is a numerical acceptance tolerance, not an additional reference decomposition. |
| banded structure-detection `atol` | 1e-12 x max\|A\| | `src/nwqlib/subroutines/block_encoding/banded.py` | Relative equality test for circulant structure under an explicit `"banded"` request. In exact arithmetic a nonzero finite factor `a` on A multiplies this window and the band magnitudes by `\|a\|`, so the same bands are selected and alpha is multiplied by `\|a\|`. A zero matrix has no bands and is refused. Passing this test does not make A a circulant, so the actual residual, including the whole diagonal of a dropped band, enters `error_bound`. Revisit with a changed precision or detector. |
| `block_encoding/core.py` | `64*eps*max(1,n,terms,bands)` | `src/nwqlib/subroutines/block_encoding/core.py` | Untuned relative candidate-comparison window for circulant classification. The exact rational residual is separately computed, and automatic routing accepts a residual within this window times the input Frobenius norm and includes its upper bound in `error_bound`. Dense automatic detection uses `64*eps*max(1,n)*max(abs(A))`. The window alone is not a certificate. Revisit condition: Changed accumulation or classification algorithm. |
| `block_encoding/core.py::_admit_pauli_plan` and `_circulant_classification_work`, planning a Pauli SELECT and the rational circulant classification of m kept terms on n qubits with b = min(m, 2**n) candidate bands | Work `C(n,m,b)` plus `mn + 2n(m + aP)`, bytes `B_held + 16mw + 2P + 32m` | `src/nwqlib/subroutines/block_encoding/core.py` | Work `C(n,m,b) = (n+32)m + 4(n+1)mb + 16b + 2b ceil(log2 max(1,b)) + 32` logical visits (label parsing, band accumulation, the m*b term-band trace calls with 4n carry transitions and four rational operations each, band records and the offset sort), added to the planning work `mn + 2n(m + aP)` for P = 2^a padded addresses: the packed X and Z masks and the dependency tests of the unsigned and signed tables. The bytes count the two packed masks of w = max(1, ceil(n/64)) words, `16mw`, and one qubit's letter codes, comparison and extracted mask words, `2P + 32m`, on top of `B_held`, the caller's data that stays in memory, each array counted once (the decomposition's labels and coefficients at `m(n+16)`, a dense input at 16D²). With classification, one chunk of the circulant trace check holds at most `max(min(mb, 2^20), b)` int64 trace entries, counted at 80 bytes each, and the larger of this and the planning bytes is checked, because the check ends before the dependency tests. Three accepted circulants on 8 to 10 qubits with up to 39,248 terms traced at most 73.6 bytes per entry (tracemalloc, Python 3.12.14, NumPy 2.5.2). Planning forms no SELECT table. The builders check the tables at `64P(n+16)` bytes and `32P max(1,n)²` work before they form them. The classifier's exact rational band coefficients are Python objects, not arrays, and are not counted. The LCHS generator selection, `src/nwqlib/algorithms/lchs/compiled_selection.py`, first checks `128*d**2` bytes against the byte limit: L and H, both coefficient vectors and a `64*d**2` reserve for completed dense child payloads and conversion copies. It holds them while each child is planned, passes them to each child as B_held, and draws on one remaining work budget for the two transforms at `32*q*d**2` each, `32*d**2` for the shared pruning and part selection, and the work that each nonempty child's planning returns. These units are proxies for the check before running, not timings or equal-cost CPU operations. Revisit condition: A changed classifier, Pauli planning arrays or SELECT table builder. |
| Dense block-encoding arrays and work | `max_work` default None (no limit), `max_bytes` default None (the physical memory) | `src/nwqlib/subroutines/block_encoding/core.py::_admit_dense_input` | Work and bytes of dense conversion, Pauli classification, SELECT table construction and dense completion, checked before each step against an explicit `max_work` and against `max_bytes`. The QSP evolution and joint-generator builders of `src/nwqlib/subroutines/qsp/evolution.py` count the exact synthesis of the dense unitaries that controlling their passes or branches makes in the same units (`_dense_synthesis.dense_synthesis_size`). Dense completion counts 8D³ for the SVD and D³ for the complement product, or D³ when the planning SVD is reused. It forms no product of the dilation with its adjoint. Planning a dense dilation without a normalization counts `src/nwqlib/_linalg_laws.py::singular_values_work` for the spectral norm `np.linalg.norm(A, 2)`, or 8D³ when the caller computes the norm with a full SVD that the completion reuses. The completion byte formula 256D²+32D counts the main completion arrays, listed in `_admit_dense_input`, as if they were alive together, an allowance for the copy that the dilation's gate can hold, and 32D² for the unpadded singular frames that the QLS padded completion keeps. Planning without completion uses an allowance of 192D²+32D bytes, more than twice the 82D² its largest counted step holds. The opaque query fallback counts `(5 + 3A) 16·4^m` bytes for each m-qubit unitary instruction of its circuit and `(5 + 3A) 64` bytes for each 2x2 matrix that a top-level `UCGate` keeps in its table (A = 1 for an uncontrolled adjoint), a coarse allowance for the stored matrices and the copies of its gate conversion and reversal (`_opaque_matrix_bytes`). Qiskit and LAPACK workspace is not counted. Revisit when the completion's arrays change or when an allowance rejects an input that the counted arrays would allow. |
| LCU size laws | `max_work` default None (no limit), `max_bytes` default None (the physical memory) | `src/nwqlib/subroutines/lcu/core.py::_admit_lcu` | Coefficient, PREP and dense SELECT sizes, checked by `_admit_lcu` before any matrix conversion or synthesis. N supplied matrices, P padded addresses, D system dimension, M=PD and m=log2(M) contribute 16P(log2(P)+1)+ND³+N(11M³+(m²+5m+256)M²) work units and 128P+64ND²+256M²+65536+N(176M²+16384) bytes. Implicit padding stores no matrices, and the synthesis terms vanish for a single term, which is not controlled. Each controlled branch is synthesized exactly from its M-square controlled matrix (`_dense_synthesis.controlled_unitary_circuit`), and `_dense_synthesis.controlled_synthesis_size` derives the terms from its recursion. The work counts the demultiplexing and block-ZXZ factorizations (8h³ for an SVD or Schur factorization of an h-square matrix and h³ for a product, as in the dense-completion formula of the dense block-encoding row), 4096 units for each of at most M²/16 two-qubit blocks, and fewer than (m²+5m)M² elementwise units. The 256M²+65536 bytes hold the controlled matrix, the factorization arrays and the gate angles of one branch at a time, and each kept branch is allowed 256 bytes for each of at most (11/16)M² instructions plus 16384 bytes of objects. Kept branches, rebuilt from their gate lists in a fresh process, held 115 to 165 bytes of resident memory per instruction for m=3 to 9, and the kept-byte formula was 1.7 to 7.0 times that memory for m=2 to 9 and 1.95 and 2.03 times at m=8 and 9. The traced working peak stayed at or below 0.48 of its formula for m=2 to 9. One branch took 0.003 s at M=32, 0.011 s at M=64, 0.044 s at M=128 and 0.21 s at M=256 on an Apple-silicon Mac with one thread. Revisit when the dense SELECT or the exact synthesis changes its construction, or when Qiskit changes how a circuit stores standard gates. |
| `analytic_prep.py::_NODE_BATCH` | 4096 nodes | `src/nwqlib/subroutines/block_encoding/analytic_prep.py` | Nodes per Clenshaw batch of the MP1 probability pass of the analytic kernel PREP, or d + 1 when the degree is larger. Each batch holds a few float arrays of this length, and the Python loop over the coefficients runs once per batch, so a small batch makes many short loops for a small degree. The batch also fixes the grouping of the `math.fsum` partial sums, so it does not depend on memory. An engineering choice, not a tolerance. Revisit with another probability pass. |
| `_multiplexors.py::_affine_angle_tolerance` | `gamma_(2k+1)` | `src/nwqlib/subroutines/_multiplexors.py` | The window is `gamma_m*S` with `gamma_m=m*eps/(1-m*eps)`, `m=2k+1` for k controls, `eps=2**-52=2u` and `S=abs(offset)+sum_q abs(c_q)`, which bounds every entry of the affine formula. `affine_angle_table_values` forms an entry as the offset plus at most k coefficients, which takes at most k rounded additions and no products, so the reconstruction differs from the exact formula by at most `k*u*S/(1-k*u)`. The window is more than four times that bound, and the remainder absorbs the rounding of the supplied table. The only code that produces such a table, the LCHS product-formula SELECT, computes each entry as `mu*t*(h+k_j*l)/r` for occurrence multiplier mu, elapsed time t, r steps, Pauli coefficients h and l and quadrature node k_j. `_attach_affine_pauli_structure` passes the affine formula with offset `(mu*t/r)*h` and coefficients `(mu*t/r)*l*w_q`, where w_q are the power-of-two bit weights of the node grid, and both computations share the rounded `mu*t`. With at most two controls every partial sum that the reconstruction forms is at most `abs(k_j*l)*mu*t/r` in magnitude, and counting the remaining roundings to first order in u bounds the disagreement by `(6*abs(h)+8*abs(k_j*l))*u*mu*t/r`. With one control (k=1) the node products and one-term sums are exact, and the bound is `(6*abs(h)+6*abs(k_j*l))*u*mu*t/r`. With three or more controls the reconstruction adds the negative sign-bit coefficient last, so a partial sum can exceed `abs(k_j*l)*mu*t/r` by a factor of up to `2**(k-1)-1`. The count is then taken against S, which bounds every partial sum, and the SELECT's four roundings, the formula's two and the reconstruction's k give at most `(k+6)*u*S` to first order. The window, about `(4k+2)*u*S`, covers these bounds for every k. It equals the one-control bound only at the node `k_j = w_0`, when h and `k_j*l` have the same sign, the SELECT's three roundings (sum, product and division) are extreme with one sign and the four roundings of the formula and its reconstruction are extreme with the other. A larger residual keeps the generic multiplexor under automatic SELECT and raises for an explicit structured request. Revisit condition: Changed arithmetic or supported precision, other code that produces such tables, or a rejected table built from exactly affine data. |

### State preparation

The `max_svd_work` of `src/nwqlib/subroutines/state_preparation/mps.py::decompose_state_to_mps`, `src/nwqlib/subroutines/state_preparation/mps_circuit.py::build_mps_circuit_state_preparation`, `LCHSCoefficientPlan.decompose_mps` and the `LCHS` Method defaults to `None`, which sets no limit. A caller who wants to bound the dense analysis sets it explicitly. Before the first TT-SVD step the limit bounds the sum of rows times columns times the smaller matrix dimension over all prospective economy SVDs. Prior rank caps affect later matrices, while current truncation does not avoid current full factors. The count does not measure FLOPs, wall time or RSS. With an explicit limit, `src/nwqlib/subroutines/state_preparation/mps_circuit.py::build_mps_circuit_state_preparation` adds to the TT-SVD work the work of the circuit construction that the largest bond dimensions the sweep can keep select, and compares the total with `max_svd_work` once, before the first SVD (`src/nwqlib/subroutines/state_preparation/mps.py::admit_mps_circuit`). A scalar needs no construction work, cores whose bond dimensions are all one need 64 units per qubit for their one-qubit gates, and other cores need at most `layers * (n - 1)` exact two-qubit syntheses (`_dense_synthesis.dense_synthesis_size`) and the layered circuit construction at those bond dimensions. LCHS planning compares the same total. The bytes of the syntheses and of the layered construction are checked against `max_bytes` before each starts. The layered construction law is `src/nwqlib/subroutines/state_preparation/mps.py::layered_construction_size`, which the direct builder and LCHS planning share ([layered MPS construction law](#layered-mps-construction-law)). The formula follows the scikit_tt sweeps, products and `mpo.dot` calls of `src/nwqlib/subroutines/state_preparation/mps_circuit.py::mps_to_circuit`, with each TT rank bounded by `min(2**min(j, n-j), 4**l b_j)` for bond dimensions `b_j` at layer l. Its untuned `32768 + 4096 n` bytes cover the Python objects of the TTs and gate MPOs. Over 180 traced constructions (random, structured, LCHS weight and pulse targets, 3 to 14 qubits, 1 to 4 layers), the formula stayed at 1.33 to 230 times the traced SVD and product work and at 1.11 to 3.8 times the traced peak bytes. The large work ratios occur where threshold truncation keeps the ranks reached for a low-rank state far below the bound. An explicit limit of 100,000,000 rejects random complex states of 12 qubits with 4 layers and of 13 or 14 qubits with 2 layers, and accepts the loader cells and the Gauss weights of the LCHS intro. The `max_bytes` default, the physical memory, separately bounds known simultaneously live arrays. Explicit reference fidelity is computed as `|<t,r>|²/(<t,t><r,r>)` by `_numerics.normalized_fidelity_with_window`, so it does not depend on how accurately the reference and the raw tensor were normalized. Its window, `((1+g)/(1-g))²(1+u)/(1-u)-1` with `g=gamma_(4D+4)`, is derived in that function from the evaluation's roundings for dimension D. The raw value is kept, and truncation or SVD error is not treated as roundoff.

| Constant | Value | Site | Reason and revisit condition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Controlled direct PREP slot bound | `4*(D-1) + 12*max(0,D-2)`, D=2**n | `src/nwqlib/_preparation_laws.py` | Two trees with D-1 rotations and D-2 CX each. An extra control costs at most 2 CX per rotation and 6 per CX, the textbook Toffoli circuit reproduced by Shende and Markov, arXiv:0803.2316v1, Fig. 1. Global phase adds no CX. The contiguous-uniform fast path has an elementary bound of 41*n-59 for n>=2, inside the complex-tree bound. Revisit when direct preparation or elementary control synthesis changes. |
| `state_preparation/mps_circuit.py` | canonical-column tolerance `1e-9` and inter-layer `ortho` threshold `1e-12` | `src/nwqlib/subroutines/state_preparation/mps_circuit.py` | Untuned local column-acceptance and tensor-compression tolerances. The local unitary completion uses at most two-qubit blocks. Tensor orthogonalization can discard singular values, so these tolerances do not give a circuit fidelity certificate. | TT-SVD `threshold=1e-14` in `state_preparation/mps.py::decompose_state_to_mps`, and `threshold=1e-14`, `num_layers=2` in `state_preparation/mps_circuit.py::build_mps_circuit_state_preparation` and the `lcu/core.py` PREP builders | `threshold=1e-14`, `num_layers=2` | `src/nwqlib/subroutines/state_preparation/mps.py`, `src/nwqlib/subroutines/state_preparation/mps_circuit.py`, `src/nwqlib/subroutines/lcu/core.py` | Each singular value at or below the threshold is dropped from the TT-SVD of the unit-norm input, keeping at least one per bond. The discarded squared weight is reported. Two disentangling layers are an untuned circuit depth whose circuit error is not evaluated at construction. The lower-level `mps_to_circuit` defaults to one layer. Revisit condition: Requested preparation accuracy, precision or tensor rank. |

#### Layered MPS construction law

`src/nwqlib/subroutines/state_preparation/mps.py::layered_construction_size` returns `(work, bytes)` of building the layered circuit from MPS cores. The work is an upper bound on the counted units below. The bytes are a coarse estimate of the dominant arrays, not a bound on process memory. The formula follows the scikit_tt calls of `mps_circuit.py::mps_to_circuit`, and of the conversion of the cores to a scikit_tt tensor train, for cores with these bond dimensions, with an upper bound on each TT rank in place of the rank a run reaches. It is defined in `mps.py`, which does not import Qiskit, so that LCHS planning can check the construction without importing Qiskit.

Ranks. Write `b_j` for the bond dimensions of n sites and `S_j = min(2**j, 2**(n-j))`. A left sweep of `TT.ortho` sets rank j+1 to at most twice rank j, and a right sweep sets rank j to at most twice rank j+1. Neither sweep raises a rank, and a truncation only lowers one, so after `ortho` every rank is at most `S_j` and at most its value before. The MPO of an extracted two-qubit gate on sites s and s+1 has rank 4 at bond s+1 and 1 elsewhere, and `mpo.dot` multiplies the ranks of the working MPS by those of the MPO. Each bond carries at most one such gate per residual pass, so the working ranks at layer l are at most `min(S_j, 4**l b_j)`.

Work, in the units of the TT-SVD work limit of `decompose_state_to_mps`:

- An SVD of an m x c core matrix, `m c min(m, c)`, counted twice because scikit_tt repeats a failed `gesdd` with `gesvd`.
- A left-sweep step with `k = min(m, c)`: `diag(s) @ vh`, `k k c`, and its `tensordot` into the next core of right rank r, `2 k c r`.
- A right-sweep step: the previous core, a `2 a x m` matrix, times U, `2 a m k`, and times `diag(s)`, `2 a k k`.
- `mpo.dot`: four products for each entry pair of MPO and MPS cores, twice the entries of the product TT.
- One unit per entry for each copy or scaling of a TT and 64 units for each 4 x 4 SVD of a gate's MPO and each unitary completion.

Each layer copies the working MPS and applies `ortho(max_rank=2)`, `norm` (a copy and a right sweep of the rank-2 result) and the scalar multiplication (two copies), and completes n local unitaries. Each of the `num_layers - 1` residual passes applies n gates, each an MPO SVD, `mpo.dot` and `ortho(threshold=1e-12)` of the product.

Bytes, 16 per entry: the target vector, the MPS cores, the right-canonical input TT and the rank-2 copies of a truncation are held throughout. On top of them come 8 entries per entry of the largest TT that the construction forms, the working TT of a layer or the product of a gate MPO and the working TT at the rank bounds above. They cover that TT, the TT it replaces, and the reshaped core, its copy and the singular factors of one sweep step. An untuned allowance of `65536 + 4096 n` bytes covers LAPACK's work arrays for these small SVDs, the gate MPOs, the Python objects of the TTs and the headers of their core arrays. Over 3 to 16 sites, bond-dimension caps 2 to 256 and 1 to 6 layers the estimate was 1.03 to 2.99 times the earlier step-by-step formula for the same arrays and zgesdd's work arrays (SciPy 1.18.1). That comparison is between the two formulas, and no memory was measured for it. Qiskit circuit objects are outside the formula, and the two-qubit syntheses have their own limit check. Cores whose bond dimensions are all one form a product state, which the builder prepares site by site without scikit_tt, 64 units per site.

### Projected eigensolver

| Constant | Value | Site | Reason and revisit condition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `DEFAULT_OVERLAP_EIGENVALUE_CUTOFF` | 1e-12 | `src/nwqlib/_projected_eigensolver.py`, used by Lanczos resolution (`src/nwqlib/algorithms/lanczos/numerical.py`) | Numerical null-space policy in Lowdin orthogonalization, not a certified input-error bound. The value is untuned, with no recorded derivation. GCiM and QFD do not read it. `FixedGCIM.overlap_cutoff`, `ADAPT.overlap_cutoff` and `QFD.overlap_cutoff` pass their own default, 1e-12, with no recorded derivation either. No revisit condition is recorded. |
| Deterministic Gram formation allowance (`gram_formation_allowance`) | `g*trace(S)/(1 - g)` with `g = sqrt(2)*gamma_(2L)` for inner products of length L | `src/nwqlib/_projected_eigensolver.py`, used by `src/nwqlib/algorithms/gcim/fixed_basis.py` (classical, L = d) and `src/nwqlib/algorithms/gcim/adapt_acquisition.py` (classical, L = 2**n) | Derived first-order bound on the spectral norm of the error of a computed Gram matrix. Each entry lies within `g*||v_i||*||v_j||` (the complex inner-product bound derived in `src/nwqlib/_validation.py` from Higham, 2002, doi:10.1137/1.9780898718027, Lemma 3.1 and Eq. (3.5)), and with `a_i = ||v_i||` the nonnegative bound matrix `g*a*a^T` has spectral norm `g*sum_i ||v_i||**2`. By Weyl's inequality a valid rank-deficient basis then has no computed overlap eigenvalue below minus this allowance and the eigensolver roundoff. It is an acceptance allowance, not a rank cutoff. Revisit if the Gram products leave binary64 or change their length. |

### Linear-algebra work laws

| Constant | Value | Site | Reason and revisit condition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `_linalg_laws.py::expm_requirements` | work `(10 + s) n**3 + 128 n**2` with `s = ceil(log2(norm))` for `norm > 1` and `s = 0` otherwise, where `norm` is the supplied upper bound on `||M||_1`, bytes `320 n**2 + 65536`, 1-norm limit `MAX_EXPONENTIAL_NORM = 2**37` | `src/nwqlib/_linalg_laws.py` | Check of one dense matrix exponential on an n-square matrix M, by `scipy.linalg.expm` or `scipy.sparse.linalg.expm`. Both SciPy routines follow Algorithm 5.1 of Al-Mohy and Higham (2009), doi:10.1137/09074721X, with up to five products for the powers that choose the Pade degree, three products and one LU solve for the degree-13 quotient, and at most `max(0, ceil(log2(||M||_1/4.25)))` squarings when `||M||_1 > 0`. A zero matrix needs no squaring. The formula counts `ceil(log2(norm))` squarings when `norm > 1` and zero otherwise, using the supplied upper bound `norm` on `||M||_1`. For `norm > 4.25`, this count is two or three larger than `ceil(log2(norm/4.25))` in exact arithmetic. The quadratic term covers the 79 products of `|M|` with a vector in the backward-error tests and 34 scaled sums. The bytes cover 20 complex n-square arrays and small objects, and traced peaks of `scipy.sparse.linalg.expm` stayed at or below 17.2 such arrays for n from 32 to 256. The work formula is a size count, not measured time. The LCHS `expm` and `closed_form` references use it with the exact 1-norm. The 1-norm limit keeps every power norm that the routines compute, of `M**k` up to k = 10 and `|M|**k` up to k = 27, below `2**999`. Above it SciPy 1.18.1's `scipy.linalg.expm` can take an infinite squaring count, so a reference is unavailable and planning rejects the request. The shared-index route of `src/nwqlib/subroutines/fermionic_circuits.py::build_generator_circuit` rejects an angle whose occupation-block exponent exceeds the 1-norm limit before it calls `scipy.linalg.expm`. Those blocks have at most five rows, so the route counts no work formula. Revisit condition: A changed routine or Pade selection in SciPy, or a workload near the work or byte cap. |
| `_linalg_laws.py::expm_multiply_requirements` | `55 ceil(N/9.9)` Taylor products, 968 norm-estimation products above N = 63.36, `entries + 7 n` units per product, bytes `64 (entries + n) + 512 n` | `src/nwqlib/_linalg_laws.py` | Check of one `scipy.sparse.linalg.expm_multiply(G, v)` on an n-square sparse G with at most `entries` stored entries and shifted 1-norm at most N. SciPy 1.18.1 implements Algorithm 3.2 of Al-Mohy and Higham (2011), doi:10.1137/100788860. Its parameter search includes m = 55 and s = ceil(alpha/9.9) with alpha at most N, which bounds the Taylor products. Above the 1-norm 63.36 of condition (3.13) for one vector it estimates the norms of the powers 2 to 9 with `onenormest`, at most 22 p products with a vector for the p-th power. Counted products stayed at or below 0.78 of the formula for tridiagonal and random Hermitian generators of order 16 to 256 and N from 0.05 to 5000. The classical QHD Schrodinger evolution and its explicit fidelity references count every call (`src/nwqlib/algorithms/qhd/method.py::restricted_sizes`). The one-hot and binary IR-product kernels make no such call. Revisit condition: A changed SciPy `expm_multiply`, or a workload near the work cap. |
| `_linalg_laws.py::seeded_norm_estimates` | seed 0 | `src/nwqlib/_linalg_laws.py` | Seed of NumPy's global generator while `scipy.sparse.linalg.expm` or `expm_multiply` estimates norms with `onenormest`. The value has no accuracy meaning. A fixed seed makes the draws, and so the chosen parameters and the rounding of the result, the same for the same input, and the caller's state is restored afterwards ([Dependency issues](dependency_issues.md#random-draws-of-the-norm-estimates)). Revisit condition: A SciPy version whose `onenormest` takes its own generator. |
| `_linalg_laws.py::singular_values_work` | `ceil(4 d**3/3) + 32 d (d + 1)` | `src/nwqlib/_linalg_laws.py` | Work of the singular values of a d-square matrix without its singular vectors, as `numpy.linalg.norm(ord=2)` and `numpy.linalg.svd(compute_uv=False)` compute them. Both run LAPACK's `gesdd` with `JOBZ='N'`, whose bidiagonal reduction takes `16 d**2 (d - d/3)` real flops for complex input and `4 d**2 (d - d/3)` for real input, `ceil(4 d**3/3)` multiply-adds in either case. Its Householder vectors, the scaling scan of `gesdd` and NumPy's copy of the input add about 4 d**2 units. The singular values of the bidiagonal matrix come from the dqds algorithm, six units per element of each transform (a division, an addition, a multiply-add, a multiplication and two comparisons). The division count that `dlasq2` reports stayed at or below 4.12 d**2 for random complex and real matrices and for matrices with clustered or graded singular values of order 4 to 128 (macOS Accelerate LAPACK), so the dqds stage took at most about 25 d**2 units, and the `32 d (d + 1)` term covers the steps after the reduction and the terms linear in d. The full SVD with both frames, counted as `8 d**3` by the dense formulas, is not computed. The checks that count this formula before the call are those of the LCHS refinement's norms (`src/nwqlib/algorithms/lchs/refinement.py::_spectral_norm`), the dense commutator norms of the fixed product-formula validation (`src/nwqlib/algorithms/lchs/time_independent_terms.py::_fixed_trotter_certificate_records`), the QLS spectral selection of a general matrix (`src/nwqlib/algorithms/qls/host_planning.py::_spectrum`) and the QLS verification reference for a missing spectrum (`src/nwqlib/algorithms/qls/verification.py::_verify`), and the block-encoding planning norm `np.linalg.norm(A, 2)` of a dense dilation (`src/nwqlib/subroutines/block_encoding/core.py::_dense_norm_work`). A dense-dilation plan whose caller supplies a full SVD for the completion keeps the `8 d**3` count. The LCHS refinement also uses this count as a proxy for the eigenvalues of a Hermitian H (`numpy.linalg.eigvalsh`, LAPACK `heevd` with `JOBZ='N'`). Its cubic term is twice the `(2/3) d**3` multiply-adds of the tridiagonal reduction, and the eigenvalue stage after that reduction has not been measured in this project, so it is assumed to fit within the extra `(2/3) d**3` and the `32 d (d + 1)` term. The count is up to about twice the actual cubic work, so an explicit `max_dense_work` can refuse this step earlier than needed. The default `None` is unaffected. Revisit condition: Another norm routine or NumPy's SVD driver, or a `dlasq2` division count above 4.6 d**2. |
| `_linalg_laws.py::hermitian_eigensystem_work` | `8 d**3 + 32 d**2` | `src/nwqlib/_linalg_laws.py` | Work of the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of a d-square Hermitian matrix by `numpy.linalg.eigh`, which runs LAPACK's `zheevd`, or `dsyevd` for real input, with `JOBZ='V'`. The tridiagonal reduction takes about (2/3) d**3 multiply-adds and the back-transformation of the eigenvectors d**2 (d - 1). The tridiagonal eigenvectors take at most (2/3) d**3 by divide and conquer above order 25 and about 4 d**3 units by implicit QL or QR iteration at order 25 and below, at two iterations per eigenvalue. The cubic term `8 d**3` equals the dense formulas' count for a full SVD and exceeds the roughly 6 d**3 of either route, and `32 d**2` covers the lower-order terms. The count exceeds the `4 d**3` that `src/nwqlib/algorithms/qls/host_planning.py::_spectrum` counts for `eigvalsh` without vectors. The QLS classical model counts it for the one original eigendecomposition of a Hermitian `A` whose factors its inverse or linear norm model reuses (`src/nwqlib/algorithms/qls/host_planning.py::original_factor_laws`) and for the eigendecomposition of the dilation of `G_t` (`src/nwqlib/algorithms/qls/host_planning.py::selected_work`), and a dense QPE power of a Hamiltonian for the shared eigendecomposition (`src/nwqlib/algorithms/qpe/powers.py::_dense_block`). Classical QPE counts it for the eigendecomposition of a Hamiltonian in its classical routine (`src/nwqlib/algorithms/qpe/method.py::_host_construction`), and `QPEVerification` for a Hamiltonian reference eigendecomposition that it computes (`src/nwqlib/algorithms/qpe/verification.py::_verify`), both through `src/nwqlib/algorithms/qpe/numerical.py::nominal_eigensystem_work`. The QLS classical model also counts `8 d**3` for each SVD with both frames, of `G_t` and of a general original `A` whose factors its inverse or linear norm model reuses. Revisit condition: NumPy's eigensolver driver, or a workload whose counted work approaches its cap. |
| `_linalg_laws.py::complex_schur_work` | `13 d**3 + 32 d**2` | `src/nwqlib/_linalg_laws.py` | QPE uses this estimate when it computes the Schur form $T$ and Schur vectors $Z$ of a $d$-dimensional unitary target with `scipy.linalg.schur(output="complex")`. LAPACK's `zgees` reduces the input to Hessenberg form (`zgehrd`, about $(5/3)d^3$ multiply-adds), forms its unitary factor (`zunghr`, about $(2/3)d^3$), then updates $T$ and $Z$ by QR iteration. The model counts single-shift sweeps at about $8dk$ units for an active block of order $k$. [LAPACK's `zhseqr`](https://github.com/Reference-LAPACK/lapack/blob/master/SRC/zhseqr.f) can use multishift QR. Two sweeps per eigenvalue give about $10.3d^3$ units including the Hessenberg stage. Golub and Van Loan (*Matrix Computations*, 4th ed., 2013, ISBN 978-1-4214-0794-4, §7.5.6, p. 391) estimate $25d^3$ real flops for the real Schur form with its orthogonal factor. [Eigen's `ComplexSchur`](https://libeigen.gitlab.io/eigen/docs-master/classEigen_1_1ComplexSchur.html) estimates $25d^3$ complex flops with the unitary factor. These estimates correspond to about $12.5d^3$ multiply-adds in the respective arithmetic. QPE counts $13d^3$ plus $32d^2$ for balancing, copies, shifts and reflectors. The number of QR iterations varies with deflation. Classical planning, classical execution and fresh `QPEVerification` use `src/nwqlib/algorithms/qpe/numerical.py::nominal_eigensystem_work`. Their nominal array estimate is $80d^2$ bytes, including the input copy, $T$ and $Z$. LAPACK chooses its own scratch space. Dense circuit synthesis uses its separate $8h^3$ convention for an SVD or complex Schur factorization (row “Dense block-encoding arrays and work”). Revisit if the Schur driver changes or a workload approaches its explicit limit. |
| `_linalg_laws.py::qr_work` | `2 m c**2 + 8 m c` | `src/nwqlib/_linalg_laws.py` | Work of the reduced QR factorization of an m x c matrix with m >= c by `numpy.linalg.qr`, which runs LAPACK's `zgeqrf`, or `dgeqrf` for real input, and forms the m x c factor Q with `zungqr` or `dorgqr`. LAPACK's operation counts for the two steps are each c**2 (m - c/3) multiply-adds, below 2 m c**2 together, and `8 m c` covers NumPy's copies of the input and of Q, the extraction of R and the Householder scalars. `QPEVerification` counts it for the QR of each eigenvalue cluster of g eigenvectors of a D-dimensional reference, with m = D and c = g, plus `16 D g` for projecting the reference on the cluster's basis, summed over the clusters and checked as one step (`src/nwqlib/algorithms/qpe/verification.py::_verify`). Revisit condition: NumPy's QR driver, or a workload whose counted work approaches its cap. |

The instance sizes in `examples/` have the rationales listed below. Revisit them when the intended example workload changes.

| H4 bond length / basis / pool / iterations / angle | 2.0 Å / STO-3G / `spin_adapted_sd` / 8 / π/4 | `examples/generators/gcim_lanczos_qpe_eigenvalue_intro.py` | At 2.0 Å CCSD lies 18 mHa below FCI, while ADAPT-GCiM reaches FCI in eight iterations of exact matrix-element evaluation. With exact probabilities one iteration as quantum circuits needs 3 circuits of at most nine qubits and the full run 136 (notebook Appendix A). Each pair circuit is reduced by applying all Pauli terms of H to its saved state, so the notebook runs the circuits on H2 at 0.74 Å instead, 3 circuits of at most five qubits. In the saved run of the notebook the chemistry setup and the eight exact iterations took 4.0 s, and the notebook states about 2 s for the H2 circuits, on a laptop with Python 3.12.14. |
| Eigenvalue notebook QCELS time step / schedule / longest time | automatic, 0.930 / `num_times=64`, all powers 0 to P / 40 per hartree | `examples/generators/gcim_lanczos_qpe_eigenvalue_intro.py` | The Hartree–Fock state has squared overlap 0.48 with the ground state, and the default QCELS schedule, powers 0 to 10, leaves an error of 121 mHa. The automatic τ = 0.9π/R uses the row-sum bound R = 3.04 Ha of the dense matrix. With `num_times=64` every longest time up to 60 per hartree gives the consecutive powers of Ding and Lin's Eq. (6), and 40 per hartree, powers 0 to 43, reaches 0.42 mHa. Spread schedules reach similar or smaller errors at 40 per hartree, 0.073 mHa with τ = 0.2 and 32 powers, but can lock onto a replica of the ground-state peak. With τ = 0.2 and 32 powers spread over 1 to 190, whose gaps are 6 or 7, a longest time of 38 per hartree returned an energy 5.16 Ha too high, near the replica spacing 2π/(6τ) = 5.24 Ha. The consecutive schedule gave no such failure at the tested longest times from 10 to 60 per hartree. Its error does not fall monotonically, for example 0.42 mHa at 40 and 6.9 mHa at 45 per hartree, and Theorem 1 (arXiv:2211.11973v2, p. 14) does not cover an overlap below 0.71, so the errors are empirical. |

| Plate grid / heaters / inverse tolerance | 4 × 4 / two / 0.01 | `examples/generators/qls_linear_system_intro.py` | Sixteen unknowns use 4 system qubits and 9 in total. The encoded inverse-gap parameter is 10.5, and the inverse polynomial has degree 55 at this tolerance. No reflection or rotation of the plate maps the two unequal heaters onto themselves, and b has a component in all 9 eigenspaces of A, so the solve uses the inverse polynomial at every distinct eigenvalue. Two equal heaters at (1, 1) and (2, 2) would reach only 6 of them. |
| Channel points / speed / diffusivity / pulse width / time / Trotter steps | 8 / 1 / 0.01 / 0.15 / 0.25 / 8 | `examples/generators/lchs_linear_dynamics_intro.py` | Upwind transport keeps concentrations nonnegative. The default kernel gives 3 system and 9 address qubits. Speed 1 makes one time unit a trip around the channel, and the final time 0.25, a quarter trip, moves the pulse two grid points while it keeps 71% of its norm. The Péclet number speed/diffusivity = 100 makes transport dominate the physical spreading. With grid spacing h = 1/8, the pulse width 0.15 ≈ h/√ln 2 gives a full width at half maximum of 2h, so the neighbors of the peak hold half its value. Plans for final times 0.25, 0.375 and 0.5 and diffusivities 0.005, 0.01, 0.02, 0.04 and 0.0625 keep 9 address qubits, so the qubit budget does not set these two values. Eight Trotter steps are an untuned illustration. The notebook separately compares finite-sum and quantum errors, which need not be ordered by this step count. |
| QHD grid points / total time / schedule / steps | 6 / 10 / quadratic, gamma 0.3 / 80 | `examples/generators/qhd_optimization_intro.py` | Two variables at six points use 12 qubits. Without a schedule the probability stays near uniform, and this schedule places 0.79 on the best grid point. |
| QHD objective / bounds | (2x²−1)² + 3x/5 + 2(y−3/10)² + 6xy/5 / ±1.2 in both variables | `examples/generators/qhd_optimization_intro.py` | The double well has minima near x = ±0.71, and the tilt 3x/5 makes the valley at negative x deeper, with the global minimum −0.81 at (−0.77, 0.53) and a local minimum 0.57 at (0.66, 0.10). The coupling 6xy/5 places the valleys at different y, so the function does not split into two one-dimensional problems. Projected gradient descent from 500 random starts in the box reaches the global minimum in 54% of runs. The box contains both valleys, and its 6 × 6 grid of interior points includes one 0.08 from the global minimum. |
| QHD constrained objective / constraint / bounds / grid points | (x−1)² + (y−1)² / x² + y² ≤ 1 / [0, 1] in both variables / 4 | `examples/generators/qhd_optimization_intro.py` | The continuous solution (1/√2, 1/√2) has f* = 3 − 2√2 ≈ 0.172 and an active constraint with multiplier √2 − 1, so the notebook can relate a grid point to it exactly. The box is the unit square, which does not depend on where the solution lies. Its 4 × 4 interior grid, with coordinates 0.2 to 0.8, uses 8 qubits and contains (0.6, 0.8) and (0.8, 0.6) on the circle, so the constraint is also active on the grid. These two points are the feasible grid minima, with objective 0.2 at distance 0.14 from the continuous solution. The 3 × 3 grid of the same box has no point on the circle. The rounds use the schedule, steps and total time of the unconstrained example. |
| LCHS scientific dimension / D / time / penalty / source rate | 4 / 4 / .005 / 1000 / 298 | `examples/generators/lchs_scientific.py` | Four-point heat flow with a constant source and imaginary boundary penalty. D = 4 and the source rate 298 are the values of the heat experiments of Schleich et al., arXiv:2506.21751v1, which place a point source of that strength at the centre element (caption of Fig. 5 and Sec. III.2.1). On four points D/h² = 36, so the paper's final time 1 would reach the steady state. The time .005 gives DT/h² = 0.18, where the slower interior mode keeps 84% of its amplitude and the source injection 1.49 is comparable with the initial norm 0.91, so the initial-state and source branches carry similar weight. The penalty 1000, λT = 5 radians, lies within the paper's numerical range, which stops at 10⁶ (Sec. III.1). With this quadrature it makes the finite-penalty error, 3.8%, comparable with the LCHS error, 2.8%. λ = 300 gives a penalty error of 11%, and λ = 3000 and 10000 make the LCHS error the larger one. The notebook performs one quantum solve and separates its error from the finite-penalty approximation using two independent references. |
| LCHS scientific k qubits / source-time nodes / total-qubit cap | 5 / 4 / 12 | `examples/generators/lchs_scientific.py` | The default 32 k nodes and four source-time nodes give 160 weighted branches in 256 address slots (basis states of the address register), for ten total qubits. The cap is the 12-qubit simulator width of the example notebooks (`docs/examples.md`). The default `DenseExact.max_select_slots=4096` applies, and the 256 address slots are within it. In the saved run the compiled circuit has about 3500 CX gates per weighted branch. |
| QLS scientific tau / time step / norm guess / construction tolerance | 1 / .1 / 4 / .001 | `examples/generators/qls_scientific.py` | Two Euler updates and two final-state copies of three collision populations form a 15-coordinate history. Every history block stays close to f(0), and ‖b‖ ≈ ‖f(0)‖, so the norm guess estimates ν = α‖x‖/‖b‖ ≈ √5 α before the run. With α = 1.88 the estimate is 4.20, and the computed ν is 4.18. Dalzell's success probability, about sin²(2θ_t) with θ_t = arctan(ν/t) (arXiv:2406.12086v2, Eqs. (7) and (17)), is 0.998 at t = 4 and 0.205 at t = 1. The relaxation changes the normalized history by about 1%, so the tolerance must be finer than that. With the notebook's construction, the QLS direction error at tolerances .01, .003 and .001 is 0.33, 0.045 and 0.017 of the no-evolution baseline for t = 4, and 1.40, 0.38 and 0.13 for t = 1. |
| QLS scientific default qubits / total-qubit cap | 10 / 12 | `examples/generators/qls_scientific.py` | Dense encoding pads the history from 15 to 16 coordinates. Encoding and shortcut ancillas give ten total qubits. The cap is the 12-qubit simulator width of the example notebooks (`docs/examples.md`). |
| Resource notebook sizes / QLS tolerance / LCHS allowances / QCELS schedule | q = 80, 90, 100, QLS tolerance .01, LCHS quadrature .005 and Strang .005, τ=.005, longest time .16, 32 positive powers, grid 256, 1024 shots per setting | `examples/generators/resource_estimation_at_scale.py` | Scalar planning at these widths forms no system statevector. The screened Poisson ring has κ=2 and inverse degree 9. The diffusion-only LCHS example uses 270 nodes, 512 addresses and 23 steps, with q100 construction work 30,047,903 and bytes 152,327,488. The explicit 20-million demonstration limit rejects this work before circuit construction. The separate .01 quadrature/.001 Strang grid has 216 nodes, 256 addresses and r49, with q80/q90/q100 work 40,641,549 / 51,150,119 / 62,893,489, all below the explicit billion-unit limit of the notebook's Appendix D. QPE's Ising and Heisenberg inputs have 200 and 300 terms at q100, and QPE's `max_work` defaults to `None`, so no work limit applies unless it is set. Powers 0 through 32 give 66 settings, and an explicit `max_work` checks the effective 257-point analysis grid and the default 4096-point grid as separate requests. The compilation appendix lets a reader inspect small circuits separately from these scalar projections. |
| Resource notebook GCiM Hamiltonian / trial states / shots | −Z^⊗q − 0.7 X^⊗q / two product states (cos θ\|0⟩ + sin θ\|1⟩)^⊗q at θ=π/8 and 3π/8 / 1024 per setting | `examples/generators/resource_estimation_at_scale.py`, `src/nwqlib/algorithms/gcim/fixed_basis.py::_sampled_construction`, `src/nwqlib/blocks/selection.py`, `src/nwqlib/_preparation_laws.py` | The two nonidentity terms form G=2 QWC groups. A and the product states are real, so the default `quadratures="auto"` measures real parts only, and the complete b=2 pencil has b(b+1)G/2=6 settings, four diagonal and two off diagonal, and 6144 shots. An uncontrolled product preparation costs zero CX. A controlled product preparation uses q copies of the one-qubit complex-tree bound, 4q CX. Group basis changes and ancilla rotations are one-qubit operations. Each off-diagonal setting is bounded by 8q CX, so the complete measurement is bounded by 16q*1024. At q=3, Qiskit 2.6.0 with basis cx,u, optimization level 0, approximation_degree=1.0 and seed 7 compiles the settings to [0,0,12,12,0,0], or 24576 CX over all shots against the bound 49152. The ratio two follows from one two-CX CRY per real product-state site and is specific to these states and compilation settings. Revisit when the preparation formula, readout construction, quadrature rule or compiler changes. |
| Resource notebook QHD grids / time / steps / start | K = 8, 16, 32 per variable / 0.001 / one first-order step, midpoint cubic-schedule weights / uniform | `examples/generators/resource_estimation_at_scale.py` | The largest one-hot circuit has 96 logical qubits, within the notebook's 80 to 100 qubit band, while binary uses 15. One step from the uniform state gives a small, reproducible workload for comparing the two encodings' circuit constructions, and the quadratic's minimum (1/4, 1/4, 1/4) lies on every grid. At K = 32 the one-hot evolution bound reaches its uninformative cap of 2, and the difference from binary reflects how each construction is bounded, not achieved accuracy. The compiled checks use K = 4 in both encodings and K = 8 in binary, which cover both binary kinetic selections with small circuits. |
